School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 4 of 250 Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Comprehensive Education Planning (CEP) is a structured, data-driven process that guides schools in assessing their needs, identifying areas for growth, setting strategic goals and implementing targeted actions to improve student outcomes. Collaboratively developed by the School Leadership Team (SLT) in accordance with the requirements of Chancellor’s Regulation A-655, the CEP meets New York State Education Department (NYSED) requirements under the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) and aligns with NYC Public Schools’ (NYCPS) system-wide priorities. By engaging all stakeholders—school leaders, teachers, families, students and community members—schools can create a data-informed, equity- focused roadmap for student success and continuous improvement. Schools should focus their CEP on the five system-wide priorities below, connecting their planning to the data discussions and conversations with stakeholders held as part of the school’s needs assessment: Priority 1: All students learn to read well Priority 2: All students are physically and emotionally safe Priority 3: All students have a high-quality academic experience Priority 4: All students graduate college and career ready and have a strong plan and pathway to economic security Priority 5: All districts and schools are more inclusive and responsive for parents and families, including having more families choose NYC Public Schools The Education Planning Process The CEP follows an equity-driven, continuous improvement cycle, rooted in the system-wide priorities. This begins with a comprehensive needs assessment of the school’s most current data to identify priority needs and root causes. Next, the SLT establishes specific, measurable, attainable, relevant, and timely— SMART goal(s) aligned to each priority and determines targets to measure progress toward meeting the annual goals. The SLT and instructional leaders define key evidence-based key strategies, professional learning activities and parent, family and community empowerment efforts. They monitor the implementation of those strategies and activities and track progress through measurable targets. Then, the SLT aligns the school-based budget with human and instructional resources to support action plans. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 5 of 250 As schools set goals, develop key strategies, and plan action steps, they provide opportunities for all children to meet NYSED’s proficient and advanced levels of student academic achievement. These include evidence based instructional strategies that address the educational needs of historically underserved populations and tackle structural inequities. Schools indicate how the plan addresses the needs of Multilingual/English Language Learners (ML/ELL) through development of the school’s Language Allocation Policy (LAP) & Title III Application (if applicable) and the Language Translation and Interpretation (LTI) Plan, that are required as part of the CEP. Schools also address the needs of Students with Disabilities (SWD) through the High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities section of the plan. SLTs also develop a plan to improve attendance and reduce chronic absenteeism. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 6 of 250 Guidance on Conducting a Comprehensive Needs Assessment Directions: Conduct a comprehensive needs assessment for each Priority. The information below provides guidance for reviewing and analyzing student and school performance trends to uncover root causes for low student achievement. 1. Review Multiple Data Sources: Use the most current and available information including, but not limited to, the following sources of formative and summative data, as applicable: Qualitative and quantitative data, including enrollment and class size trends 2024-25 ESSA Accountability Data Instructional Leadership Framework (ILF), Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) Inventories, and NYC School Survey results Quality Review Reports, NYSED and NYCPS CSI or A/TSI Support Visits, Receivership Quarterly Reports, and Diagnostic Tool for School and District Effectiveness Framework (DTSDE) NYC School Quality Guide, Insight, New Visions, NWEA MAP and/or other formative assessment data, IEP Review Survey Data, Progress on IEP Goals Data, Program (SESIS-STARS Linkage) Reports, Mandated (Related) Services Reports, and NYCPS Tools for Understanding Your Data for student subgroup information 2. Analyze Data Trend(s) to Identify Areas of Strength and Need: Identify data trends by grade, cohort, and content area(s) to surface gaps and inequities. Compare data sets to identify instructional and school practices that are impacting student performance. Guiding questions to consider: What does the data reveal about improving and declining student performance trends within grades, cohorts, and student subgroups? What inequities are evident between highest and lowest performing student subgroups? Which subgroup(s) scored at Level 1 based on NYSED ESSA accountability indicators? What does the data suggest regarding the effectiveness (or ineffectiveness) of current school programs, practices, and/or strategies/initiatives? What areas may need to be strengthened? Are there any differences in student performance between program delivery models for ML/ELL and SWD? (e.g., for special education: special class, ICT, SETSS, related services only; for ML/ELL: Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) vs. Dual Language, etc.) How does delivery of instruction factor into data trends? What are the implications for teacher quality and professional learning? What inequities in special education referral and school disciplinary practices (e.g. suspensions, removals) are evident between student subgroups with IEPs? 3. Identify Root Cause(s): After analyzing the data, engage in a process for uncovering root causes or contributing factors for low student and school performance. Develop theories or hypotheses to explain DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 7 of 250 why these needs exist and why your school is getting its current outcomes. Root causes could be related to gaps and inequities in areas such as curriculum & instruction, school systems & structures, student support services, school climate & safety, and family & community engagement. Focus on the causes, rather than the symptoms. Use the 5 Whys Method or other methods for identifying root causes. Consider the questions below: What theories or hypotheses explain the current student and school outcomes? Are there root causes specific to certain subgroups, grades, cohorts, or content area(s)? Additional resources and supports are available below. Use this CEP Needs Assessment Worksheet to help your SLT and stakeholders identify appropriate data sources, data trends, root causes and priority needs. For guidance and best practices on engaging stakeholders in a comprehensive needs assessment and root cause analysis, see this deck. A current list of all CEP/Title I resources can be found on the iPlan Portal Public Resources Page. Under the Federal Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), NYS Education Law and Commissioner’s Regulations, stakeholder participation in the development of the CEP must include multiple constituencies who represent the school community. Provide your school’s mission statement: The Louis Armstrong School fosters the linguistic and academic development of all scholars in our learning community. We establish a linguistically and culturally rich community of learning where students are encouraged to take intellectual risks in a compassionate, inclusive, safe, and rewarding environment. Our instructional practices utilize a variety of research based approaches to provide meaningful, authentic instruction appropriate for the diverse needs of our student body. Providing multiple paths to success, incorporating scaffolds, and using rich and varied resources helps students strengthen their understanding of the content across all subject areas. Together, we adapt, create, engage, nurture, advocate, support, serve, and love. We stand firm that all students can achieve success and celebrate the collective and individual milestones of our children. Develop a narrative summary that includes contextual information about your school’s community and its unique/important characteristics and partnerships: Include demographic information about your school’s student population. Located in the heart of Corona, Queens, P.S. 143Q – The Louis Armstrong School – is a dynamic elementary school that celebrates cultural richness, community, and academic excellence. Serving more than 1,500 students in grades Kindergarten through 5, the school fosters a warm, inclusive environment where every child is empowered to learn, grow, and lead. With 70% of students identified as Multilingual Learners and 19% as Students with Disabilities, PS 143Q embraces the diversity of its student body as a driving force for innovation and equity. This diversity shapes the school’s mission: to provide high-quality, inclusive instruction that meets the needs of every learner. At the core of PS 143Q’s instructional vision is a commitment to research-based, data-driven practices. In the 2024– 2025 school year, the school continues to implement Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura and enVision Math, two comprehensive programs that prioritize language development, foundational literacy, and conceptual understanding in math. These programs are supported by a targeted focus on phonemic awareness, decoding, and academic vocabulary, ensuring all students build strong foundations in reading, writing, and mathematical thinking. Learning at PS 143Q extends beyond the classroom. The school’s state-of-the-art library, home to over 11,000 multilingual books, invites students to explore the world through literature. A robust dual-language program—offered in all grades—nurtures biliteracy and fosters deep cultural competence, allowing students to celebrate their heritage while preparing for a global future. PS 143Q thrives because of its strong collaborative culture. Teachers, support staff, administrators, and families work in unison to promote academic achievement, social-emotional wellness, and community pride. Through regular DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 16 of 250 communication, workshops, and schoolwide events, families are active partners in their children’s learning journey. The school’s alignment with NYC Department of Education priorities ensures that equity, excellence, and engagement remain at the forefront. In every hallway and classroom, PS 143Q embodies its values: diversity, curiosity, and high expectations. It is a joyful and rigorous learning community where every child’s potential is honored and nurtured. Briefly summarize the key initiatives your school will implement this school year. This school year, PS 143Q will implement several key initiatives to support student achievement and equity. These include the continued rollout of Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura and enVision Math to strengthen literacy and math instruction, a schoolwide focus on foundational phonics and vocabulary development, and targeted support for Multilingual Learners and Students with Disabilities through inclusive, data-driven practices. The school will also deepen its dual language program across all grades and expand family engagement efforts to strengthen the home- school connection. More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Implement targeted small-group foundational literacy instruction during WIN (What I Need) time to strengthen students’ phonemic awareness, decoding, and comprehension skills based on mCLASS data. Root Cause Addressed: Tier 1 instruction and intervention time have not been consistently differentiated to meet students’ foundational literacy needs, particularly for early readers and multilingual learners. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Use BOY, MOY, and EOY mCLASS data to form and adjust targeted small-group instruction during WIN time, focusing on foundational literacy skills (phonemic awareness, phonics, and fluency). Provide ongoing professional learning for teachers on implementing effective small-group instruction aligned to the Science of Reading. Develop WIN group plans that integrate HMH Into Reading routines and decodable text practice to reinforce core instruction. Support teachers in embedding language scaffolds—such as visuals, vocabulary routines, and sentence stems— during small-group literacy lessons to support ELLs. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 27 of 250 Expand classroom libraries and small-group materials, including bilingual and Spanish decodables, to support skill transfer and family engagement in literacy at home. Key Strategy 2 (Optional): Implement schoolwide structures that promote academic discourse and oral language development across all content areas through consistent use of speaking protocols, targeted small-group instruction, and data-driven reflection cycles. Root Cause Addressed: While students are making strong gains in language proficiency, sustaining growth toward the Commanding level requires continued emphasis on oral language practice, discourse structures, and teacher calibration in scoring and instruction. Strengthening these structures will deepen students’ ability to communicate with academic precision and fluency. Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Provide PD on Accountable Talk, Collaborative Conversations, and other discussion protocols aligned to Danielson 3b. Introduce schoolwide rubrics aligned to NYSESLAT Speaking proficiency levels (Entering ? Commanding). During common planning, teachers co-develop language objectives and sentence frames aligned to unit content and language demands. Conduct classroom walkthroughs focused on evidence of student discourse and language scaffolds. Review speaking rubric and i-Ready data at instructional leadership and grade-level meetings to identify trends and next steps. Implementation Monitoring: Indicate your primary structures to progress monitor implementation of key strategies and action steps. Include specific data/evidence you will collect and use to determine progress and impact on instruction, student learning, and achievement. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 28 of 250 Implementation of small-group foundational literacy instruction during WIN time will be monitored through multiple structures and data sources at consistent intervals throughout the school year: Instructional Walkthroughs: Conducted bi-weekly by administrators and coaches using the NYC Reads Implementation Tool to monitor fidelity to small-group instruction, foundational skill routines, and language supports. Data Reviews: Held monthly during grade-level team meetings to analyze mCLASS progress monitoring data, decodable reading checks, and formative assessments to adjust WIN groups and instructional focus. Coaching and Feedback Cycles: Conducted ongoing (at least monthly) by the Literacy Coach and AP to provide targeted feedback on small-group instruction and Science of Reading practices. Collaborative Planning Sessions: Scheduled bi-weekly for ENL, AIS, and classroom teachers to align supports, review student progress, and plan next steps for multilingual learners. This limits the reach and sustainability of trauma-informed and proactive behavior supports. Launch staff training cohorts: Begin the first cohort of TCIS participants in Fall 2025, ensuring a balance of teachers, paraprofessionals, and related service providers. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 107 of 250 Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Academic Support NYCPS Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) align with NYSED guidelines for providing Academic Intervention Services (AIS) to students who have not yet met state standards or are considered to be at- risk of not meeting state standards. The Multi-tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) framework, organizes supports into three tiers: Tier 1 provides schoolwide, high-quality programming for all students, including SWDs; Tier 2 offers data informed small-group interventions for at-promise students; and Tier 3 delivers intensive, individualized support for students with persistent needs. This facilitates the strategic planning and necessary resource alignment to ensure their school community appropriately and adequately serves all students well, in partnership with students and families. MTSS identifies the academic support services to be provided and ensures that students receive Response to Intervention (RtI) and Positive Behavioral Interventions and Support (PBIS) strategies as needed. See NYSED’s memo to view the criteria for determining AIS services. The Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) offers a wide range of evidence-based Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions and supports for student social-emotional, behavioral, physical and mental wellness, with particular attention to the unique needs of students in Temporary Housing and/or Foster Care, who may face additional challenges related to school stability, trauma and access to educational resources. A culturally responsive-sustaining approach to intervention embeds culturally relevant texts and historical narratives into literacy and social studies interventions, ensuring students receive the support that reflects their identities and lived experiences, increasing engagement and achievement. Use the charts below and a Multi-Tiered System of Support (MTSS) to match academic, social/emotional, behavioral and instructional resources with student needs to provide targeted support for all learners, especially at-risk students who experience difficulty meeting state standards, including those living in Temporary Housing and/or residing in Foster Care placements. Indicate specialized materials, technology- based tools and/or extra instruction, including Expanded Learning Time that supplement school-wide systems & supports and/or classroom level supports. Ensure that interventions address the unique social- emotional, behavioral and academic challenges faced by culturally and linguistically diverse students, as well as those impacted by housing instability and child welfare system involvement. Our school communicates the essential components of MTSS through family workshops, informational flyers, and individual meetings during parent-teacher conferences. These communications explain the tiered system of support, how data informs decisions, and the school-wide strategies used to address academic, social-emotional, and behavioral needs. We also share this information through our school website, newsletters, and Back-to-School Night presentations to ensure all families have access to this foundational knowledge. Describe how families receive updates on the progress of their child(ren) who receive Tier 2 targeted, or Tier 3 intensive interventions. Families of students receiving Tier 2 or Tier 3 interventions are provided with regular progress updates through multiple formats including monthly progress monitoring reports, parent-teacher conferences, and scheduled check- ins with intervention providers or support staff. These updates include specific data points (e.g., reading fluency scores, behavioral checklists), intervention goals, and adjustments made to support student growth. All communications are delivered in the family's preferred language to ensure clarity and access. Describe how families are involved during decision making regarding the progress of students receiving Tier 3 intensive intervention. Families of students receiving Tier 3 intensive interventions are active participants in the decision-making process through regularly scheduled parent teacher conferences. During these meetings, the intervention team reviews data, discusses progress, and collaboratively plans next steps with the family. Parents are encouraged to share insights about their child’s strengths, challenges, and needs. Interpreters are provided as needed to support inclusive and meaningful collaboration in each decision. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 112 of 250 Students in Temporary Housing (STH) Support As included in your school’s Office of School and Youth Development Consolidated Plan-STH Section and in accordance with the federal McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act and Chancellor's Regulation A- 780, schools must identify, serve, and report on students living in temporary housing (STH). All Title I and non-Title I schools receive a Title I allocation for STH students. The Students in Temporary Housing (STH) website provides guidance on STH pupils and how these funds can be used to meet their needs, as well as restrictions. For more information on acceptable uses of the mandated Title I, Part A set-aside to support your STH population, please refer to the Title I STH Funds Toolkit. Identify the number of Students in Temporary Housing who are currently attending your school and provide a breakdown of their temporary housing status. Since the number of STH children can change regularly, schools must carefully track this number to determine whether there has been an increase in need. Number of students in temporary housing who are: Doubled up (with friends or relatives because they cannot find or afford housing): Living in a shelter or transitional shelter: Living in a hotel/motel: Other *Note: In keeping with established practices regarding personally identifiable information, use the letter “s” to identify a number less than five (5). Currently PS143Q has 427 students in temporary housing. After a careful analysis of historical demographic, attendance and achievement data for your STH population, what trends do you notice, and how can these trends inform the services and support provided for this population? After a careful analysis of historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for our Students in Temporary Housing (STH) population, several key trends have emerged. STH students at PS143Q consistently demonstrate higher rates of chronic absenteeism compared to their housed peers, particularly among those living in shelters or frequently changing addresses. Academic achievement data shows that STH students are more likely to perform below grade level in both literacy and math, with gaps widening over time without targeted intervention. Additionally, social-emotional and behavioral concerns are more prevalent, likely due to the instability and trauma often associated with housing insecurity. These trends inform the need for a multi-pronged approach that prioritizes consistent attendance outreach, access to academic interventions during the school day, trauma-informed counseling services, and targeted family engagement efforts. By addressing both academic and non-academic barriers, the school can create a more stable and supportive environment that promotes continuity, achievement, and overall well-being for students in temporary housing. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 113 of 250 Describe the services you are planning to provide to the STH population. How were those services selected to best meet students’ needs? How will you (or did you) utilize the STH Title I Survey to select services aligned to need? To support our Students in Temporary Housing (STH) population, PS143Q is planning to implement a range of academic, social-emotional, and basic needs services tailored to the challenges these students face. Planned services include: Academic support through targeted small-group intervention during the school day for literacy and math Attendance outreach and early intervention through our MTSS Attendance Team to reduce chronic absenteeism Access to school supplies, uniforms, hygiene kits, and emergency clothing for new and returning STH students On-site counseling and check-ins with a dedicated social worker trained in trauma-informed practices Family outreach and case management support to assist with transportation, housing documentation, and connection to community-based resources Translation and interpretation services to ensure families with limited English proficiency can fully participate in school decisions and services These services were selected based on an analysis of school-level data and trends, and were prioritized in response to feedback collected from families through the STH Title I Survey. The survey highlighted needs such as transportation challenges, access to basic school items, and the desire for more frequent communication about academic progress. By using this input alongside data on attendance, academic achievement, and mobility, we are able to offer responsive supports aligned to the real experiences and needs of our STH students and their families. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 114 of 250 Prioritizing Students in Foster Care As included in your school’s Office of School and Youth Development Consolidated Plan-Child Child Abuse and Neglect Intervention and Prevention School Plan and in accordance with the federal Education Law 3244 – Education of Children in Foster Care (Effective 2018) establishes the rights of students placed, or moved, in foster care to: Continue attending school of origin if determined to be in their best interest, or, if not, attend school near foster care placement, Immediate enrollment, and Transportation, if necessary to attend school of origin Please respond to the questions below. How many students in foster care are currently enrolled at your school? Provide a detailed breakdown of the social-emotional and academic supports available to them. Currently, three students in foster care are enrolled at PS143Q. To support the unique needs of students in foster care, the school provides a range of targeted social-emotional and academic supports, including: Dedicated social-emotional support through our guidance counselor and school social worker, who conduct regular check-ins and provide trauma-informed counseling services. Priority access to academic intervention services, including small-group instruction in literacy and math based on assessment data and teacher input. Case management and coordination with foster agencies and external service providers to ensure alignment between school supports and outside care. Attendance monitoring and outreach to ensure consistency and identify early signs of disengagement. Access to basic needs resources, including uniforms, school supplies, and meal support, to eliminate barriers to school readiness and participation. Inclusive family engagement strategies, including outreach to foster families and caregivers, and translation services where needed to ensure clear communication. Our staff is trained to maintain confidentiality while creating a supportive, stable school environment that promotes continuity, belonging, and academic success for students in foster care. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 115 of 250 What targeted supports are in place for students in foster care, including those newly transitioning into the foster care system? How does your school ensure these students receive the necessary interventions to promote stability and success? At PS143Q, we have several targeted supports in place to ensure students in foster care—including those newly transitioning into the system—receive the interventions they need to promote stability and success. Upon enrollment or notification of a change in a student's foster care status, the school immediately activates a support plan that includes: A welcome meeting with the school counselor or social worker to assess immediate needs and begin building a trusting relationship A comprehensive academic review to determine if the student requires Tier 2 or Tier 3 academic interventions in literacy or math Social-emotional check-ins and ongoing counseling sessions as needed, using a trauma-informed approach to help students process transitions Access to school supplies, uniforms, and meals to ensure basic needs are met and reduce stressors that may impact learning Close coordination with foster agencies and caseworkers, including sharing attendance and academic progress, and participating in team meetings as appropriate Prioritization for after-school programs and enrichment opportunities, to build routine, connection, and a sense of belonging To promote long-term success, we also ensure these students are monitored through our MTSS framework, with regular reviews of attendance, academic progress, and behavioral data to quickly identify and respond to emerging needs. Our staff receives ongoing training to maintain sensitivity and confidentiality while creating a nurturing and inclusive environment for all students in foster care. After analyzing historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for students in foster care, what trends have emerged? How can these insights be used to improve the services and support provided to this population? After analyzing historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for students in foster care at PS143Q, several key trends have emerged. Students in foster care often experience higher rates of absenteeism, particularly during times of placement changes or transitions between schools. Academically, many students enter with gaps in foundational skills, especially in literacy and math, likely due to interrupted schooling and limited access to consistent academic support. Social-emotionally, these students often demonstrate increased signs of anxiety, difficulty with peer relationships, and challenges adjusting to routines, which can impact classroom engagement and performance. These insights highlight the need for proactive and coordinated services that address both academic and social- emotional needs. To improve support for this population, our school is focused on: Rapid-response enrollment procedures and academic screenings to minimize learning disruptions DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 116 of 250 Early and ongoing counseling services that address trauma, transitions, and self-regulation Consistent communication with foster care agencies to align school-based supports with external case plans Regular progress monitoring through our MTSS system to adjust interventions based on attendance, behavior, and academic data Staff training in trauma-informed practices to foster a safe, stable, and inclusive learning environment By using these data trends to inform service planning and resource allocation, we can better meet the complex needs of students in foster care and create a school environment that supports their long-term stability, growth, and success. What specific strategies does your school implement to ensure students in foster care, particularly those in transitional grades, receive targeted Social-Emotional Learning (SEL) support? How do these strategies help address academic and behavioral challenges? At PS143Q, we implement several targeted Social-Emotional Learning (SEL) strategies to support students in foster care, with a particular focus on those in transitional grades such as Kindergarten, Grade 2 (transition from early to upper elementary), and Grade 5 (preparing for middle school). These strategies are designed to promote emotional stability, build resilience, and reduce the academic and behavioral challenges often experienced during periods of transition. Key strategies include: Individual and small-group SEL sessions led by our school counselor and social worker, focused on self-regulation, building relationships, and managing change. Students in foster care are prioritized for these services. Check-in/check-out systems that provide daily emotional support and goal setting with a trusted adult, fostering a sense of consistency and belonging. Social stories and transition toolkits for younger students to prepare them for changes in classroom expectations, routines, or school environments. Peer buddy programs and structured recess groups to help build positive peer connections and reduce feelings of isolation. Regular collaboration between the SEL team and teachers to ensure strategies are embedded in the classroom setting and adjusted based on individual needs. Family and agency partnerships to align school-based SEL efforts with external mental health or wraparound services. These strategies help students in foster care build emotional resilience, reduce behavioral incidents, and increase classroom engagement, which in turn supports academic progress. By providing consistent, trauma-informed SEL supports, our school helps foster a safe and supportive environment where students can thrive despite personal and structural challenges. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 117 of 250 Section 7: Parent and Family Engagement Policy and School-Parent Compact Section 7: Parent and Family Engagement Policy and School-Parent Compact Parent and Family Engagement Policy Directions: The Parent and Family Engagement Policy should describe how your school will plan and implement effective parent and family engagement activities and/or strategies to improve student academic achievement and school performance. The School-Parent Compact (SPC) is a component of the Parent and Family Engagement Policy (PFEP) that outlines how parents, the entire school staff, and students will share this responsibility. The activities and/or strategies included in your school's Parent and Family Engagement Policy should align with current Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) goals for improving student achievement and should be developed or updated with input and feedback from parent leaders and the Parent Coordinator. In addition, if the school community will be engaged in central parent engagement initiatives, such as Parent Academy, which will provide training for school communities to help strengthen family-school partnerships, please be sure to include these activities in the school's policy. In Title I schools, the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) represents the Title I parent community in supporting parent and family engagement. The Title I PAC Chairperson will serve in a consultative role with the SLT representing the school’s Title I parents. Schools are encouraged to use the sample Parent and Family Engagement and SPC templates below (which meet federal Title I parent and family engagement requirements) as guidance for updating the school's current policy. Parent and Family Engagement Policy Educational research shows a positive correlation between effective parental engagement and student achievement. The overall aim of this policy is to develop a parent and family engagement policy that will establish expectations and objectives for meaningful parent and family engagement in the school community. P.S. 143Q, in compliance with Section 1116 of Title I, Part A of the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), is responsible for creating and implementing a parent and family engagement policy to strengthen the connection and support of student achievement between the school and the families. The school's policy is designed to keep parents informed by actively involving them in planning and decision-making in support of the education of their children. Parents are encouraged to actively participate on the School Leadership Team, Parent Association (or Parent-Teacher Association), and Title I Parent Advisory Council as trained volunteers and welcomed members of the school community. Explanation/Background: Title I Schoolwide Program schools are expected to use the flexibility available to them to integrate services and programs with the aim of enhancing the entire educational program and helping all students reach proficient and advanced levels of achievement. In addition to coordinating and integrating services, Schoolwide Program schools may combine most Federal, State and local funds to provide those services. By consolidating funds from Federal, State, and local sources, a Schoolwide Program school can address its needs using all its available resources. This gives a school more flexibility in how it uses available resources to meet the identified needs of all its students. Consolidating funds in a Schoolwide Program means that a school treats the funds it is consolidating like they are a single “pool” of funds. In other words, the funds from the contributing programs in the school lose their individual identity and the school has one flexible pool of funds. The school uses funds from this consolidated Schoolwide pool to support any activity of the Schoolwide Program without regard to which program contributed the specific funds used for a particular activity – so long as the use of funds meets the intent and purposes of the programs from which the funds originate. To consolidate funding in a Schoolwide Program, the school does not literally need to combine funds in a single account or pool with its own accounting code. Rather, the word “pool” is used conceptually to convey that a Schoolwide Program school has the use of all consolidated funds available to it for the dedicated function of operating a Schoolwide Program without regard to the identity of those funds. Consolidating Federal funds in a Schoolwide Program has the following additional advantages: Consolidating Federal funds eases the requirements for accounting for funds from each specific program separately, because a Schoolwide school is not required to distinguish among funds received from different sources when accounting for their use. A school that consolidates Federal funds in its Schoolwide Program is not required to meet most of the statutory and regulatory requirements of the specific Federal programs included in the consolidation (e.g., semi-annual time and effort reporting for Title I). However, the school must ensure that it meets the intent and purposes of the Federal programs included in the consolidation so that the needs of the intended beneficiaries are met. 2. The intent and purposes of the Federal programs indicated on the chart above (Part 4c of this section) are as follows: Title I, Part A – Schoolwide Programs: To upgrade the entire educational program in the school in order to improve the academic achievement of all students, particularly the lowest-achieving students. This includes provision of services for Students in Temporary Housing (STH). Title I 1003(a), School Improvement: Comprehensive Support and Improvement (CSI), Additional Targeted Support and Improvement (ATSI) and Targeted Support and Improvement (TSI) Funding: DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 129 of 250 Support implementation of school improvement plans that aims to improve instruction and address the identified needs Title II, Part A: Supplementary funding to improve student academic achievement by reducing class size in grades K, 1, 2, and 3, with an emphasis on grades with an average register greater than 20. If space is not available to form additional classes, funds may support push-in teacher(s) to supplement the instructional program. For schools that choose to reduce class size, priority must be given to reducing class size in general education settings and must demonstrate the following: Reduce class sizes to 15-18 students or fewer (including the use of co-teaching and floating teachers that bring the student to teacher ratio to one teacher per 15-18 students); Be accompanied by a rigorous curriculum, effective teachers, and a sustained, job-embedded professional development plan. Title III, Part A: To help ensure that children with limited English proficiency become proficient in English, develop high academic attainment in English, and meet the same challenging State academic content and achievement standards in the core academic subjects that all other children are expected to meet. Another purpose of this program is to increase the capacity of schools to establish, implement and sustain high-quality language instruction programs and English language development programs that assist schools in effectively teaching students with limited English proficiency. Title III, Part A is also designed to promote the participation of parents and communities of limited English proficient children in English language instruction programs. Title III Immigrant: Supplementary and enhanced services to immigrant students, in the areas of English language acquisition and content area achievement. Immigrant children and youth are defined as individuals who are aged 3 through 21, have not been attending school in any one or more states for more than three (3) full academic years, and were not born in any state. “State” means the 50 states of the United States, the District of Columbia, and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. Children born to U.S. citizens abroad, the U.S. Virgin Islands, or any other U.S. territory that is not D.C. or Puerto Rico are to be considered immigrants. Title IV, Part A: Title IV funds are for schools that are among those with the greatest needs, have the highest numbers of students from low-income families, are identified for CS, ATSI or TSI, or are identified as persistently dangerous public schools. Allowable uses of funds under well-rounded education, safety and health, and educational technology include direct services for students, professional development for teachers and administrators, and supplemental educational resources. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 143 of 250 Describe your English as a New Language (ENL), and if applicable Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE), and Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) programs. For each program, include: How stand-alone ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How integrated ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How students are grouped (e.g., block [class travels together as a group], ungraded [all students regardless of grade are in one class], heterogeneous [mixed proficiency levels], homogenous [proficiency level is the same in one class]). a. English as a New Language (ENL) Stand-alone ENL: During Stand-Alone instruction, ENL teachers develop English language skills so that students can succeed in core content classes. In Stand-Alone English as a New Language (ENL) classes, students receive ENL services and English language development instruction from a New York State certified teacher of English to Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) in order to succeed in core content areas. As per CR Part 154 to meet the minimum requirement, teachers service students at the ENTERING level for 180 minutes and students at the EMERGING level for 90 minutes at a separate location. The ML/ELLs serviced in the ENL Stand-Alone classroom settings receive all instruction by a dually certified (General Education and ESOL) classroom teacher. As per CR Part 154 to meet the minimum requirement, all ENL teachers service all students at the ENTERING level for 180 minutes and all students at the EMERGING level for 90 minutes within the classroom setting. According to the September 2025 RFSF report, our school is composed of 163 students who scored at the ENTERING and 208 students who scored at the EMERGING level on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT and the Fall 2025 NYSITELL. Within a Stand-Alone ENL class, students are grouped homogeneously when working in smaller groups in order to meet students' specific linguistic needs. ENL providers utilize student data and teacher observations to determine student grouping. During our Common Planning periods, teachers utilize analyzed ENL data by modality to create purposeful student groupings. These groupings are also informed by ongoing teacher observations of student performance and language use in the classroom. Teachers leverage students' strengths to build linguistic capacity in modalities that require additional support. Based on the linguistic demands of the task and students’ proficiency levels in each modality, groups are strategically formed, scaffolded appropriately, and provided with targeted resources to support language development. In addition, for EMERGING level students, instructional decisions are made regarding flexibility in Stand-Alone versus Integrated units of study, based on continuous assessment and student progress in English Language acquisition. This allows teachers to refine lessons and scaffold instruction effectively. During Stand-Alone instruction, ENL teachers focus on English language acquisition through the use of clearly defined language objectives, which support the development of listening, speaking, reading, and writing skills in English. These objectives are aligned with the students' language proficiency levels and are designed to help students access academic content while advancing their command of English. Integrated ENL: In accordance with Commissioner’s Regulation (CR) Part 154, Integrated English as a New Language (ENL) instruction is designed to provide Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners (MLs/ELLs) with English DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 144 of 250 language development while simultaneously supporting access to core content instruction. Integrated ENL is delivered through a co-teaching model, in which a New York State certified teacher of English to Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) collaborates with the classroom teacher to implement language development strategies within the classroom. In an Integrated English as a New Language (ENL) classroom, ML/ELLs receive their mandated units of study according CR Part 154. ENTERING and EMERGING level students receive 180 minutes of integrated services in ELA, TRANSITIONING level students receive 90 minutes of integrated services in ELA and EXPANDING level students receive 180 minutes of integrated services in ELA. After students become COMMANDING as measured by the NYSESLAT, they receive 90 minutes of integrated ENL in ELA for two additional years. The integrated units of study are designed to continue to support students' English language acquisition during ELA and core content instruction. According to the September 2025 RFSF report, our school is composed of 163 students who scored at the ENTERING, 208 students who scored at the EMERGING level, 246 students who scored at the TRANSITIONING level, and 234 students who scored at the EXPANDING level on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT and the Fall 2025 NYSITELL. In addition, we also have 92 students who scored at the COMMANDING level on the Spring 2024 and 2025 NYSESLAT. Within integrated ENL units of study, students are grouped heterogeneously during whole group and small group instruction utilizing student data and teacher observations. In addition, classes are grouped heterogeneously, consisting of ML/ELLs, FELLs, and Non-ELLs. Thus, the ML/ELL levels in the integrated ENL classes range from Entering to Commanding. During our Common Planning periods, teachers analyze data to create strategic partnerships and triads for listening and speaking activities. For example an Entering level student may be paired with a Transitioning level student or a triad with an Entering, Transitioning and Expanding students. They utilize ENL data by modality to form purposeful student groupings that support language development. These groupings are also informed by ongoing teacher observations of student performance and language use in the classroom. In instruction, Commanding-level students serve as language models to support the English language acquisition of their peers. Differentiated language scaffolds are provided based on students' ENL levels and teacher observations. During Integrated ENL periods, these scaffolds are further tailored to meet the linguistic needs of each group, promoting meaningful access to content and continued language acquisition. Instruction is designed to embed language objectives alongside content objectives, ensuring that students are developing English language skills in reading, writing, listening, and speaking as they engage with grade-level content. Teachers also utilize students’ home language strategically as a bridge to learning, supporting deeper comprehension and building on existing linguistic assets. b. Bilingual Education (If applicable) Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE): Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program: Currently we have six Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) Spanish classes. These are 12:1:1 Special Education classes in grades K-5. English and Spanish instruction is delivered by a teacher who is certified in Special Education and who holds a Bilingual (Spanish) certification or extension as well. ML/ELLs are placed in TBE classes according to the language of instruction determined by each individual students’ IEPs. Within these specific classes, students are grouped homogeneously when working in smaller groups in order to meet students' specific linguistic needs in addition to their IEP goals. The students in the TBE programs transfer home language skills from Spanish to English. Students receive 70% of instruction in Spanish and 30% instruction in English at the beginning of the school year. As the year progresses and DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 145 of 250 the students become more proficient in English, the percentage of instruction in Spanish decreases as the English instruction increases. Instruction is also differentiated for individual students according to their ML/ELL proficiency level. For example, the ratio of home language to English for Entering students is 70/30; for Emerging students is 60/40; for Transitioning students is 50/50; and for Expanding students is 30/70. In TBE programs, teachers also take several considerations when determining which language is best to teach certain concepts. Teachers consider carefully the rigor of instruction, student familiarity with concepts in the home language and English, and the academic language required for using and mastering concepts. When determining the amount of home language and English language used in instruction, teachers consider a number of factors including, but not limited to students’ proficiency levels, educational backgrounds, content knowledge, and home language literacy skills. This allows for differentiated instruction that meets the individual student’s needs. Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE): Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) program: Currently we have 12 Dual Language General Education classes and 6 Dual Language ICT classes. Dual Language (DL) General Education Classes consist of two classes in each grade. Each class consists of one teacher who holds a Bilingual license. The Dual Language teachers employ a 50/50 model where Spanish and English instruction is delivered equally. Classes are grouped heterogeneously, consisting of ML/ELLs, FELLs, and Non-ELLs. Thus, the ML/ELL levels in the Dual Language classes range from Entering to Commanding. All ML/ELLs receive instruction for the mandated units of study according to their proficiency level by the classroom teachers. The classroom teacher integrates ENL strategies in the content area and, in addition, provides Stand-Alone ENL instruction for Entering and Emerging students. Non-ELLs are placed in this setting due to the fact that their parents have expressed a desire for their child to achieve proficiency in both languages. The Non-ELLs can be from Spanish speaking homes, or from homes where English or another language is spoken primarily. In this specific program, students are immersed in instruction that supports their home language and culture. Our Dual Language Integrated Co-Teaching (ICT) Classes consist of one class per grade ranging from grades K-5. Instruction will be delivered by a teacher who has a Special Education license, as well as a teacher who holds a Common Branch/Bilingual license. In some classes, one of the classroom teachers may also be TESOL certified. The teachers provide their ML/ELLs with the necessary strategies to meet students’ individual needs as well as Special Education students’ IEP goals. In addition, both teachers provide students with the mandated instructional minutes according to their ML/ELL proficiency level through a collaborative co-teaching approach. The classes consist of about 30 students, in which there is a maximum of 12 students with an Individualized Educational Plan (IEP) whose language of instruction is Spanish, according to their IEPs. The remainder of the class consists of a mixture of ELLs and Non-ELLs from Spanish speaking homes, or from homes where English or another language is spoken primarily. Students without IEPs are placed in this setting due to the fact that their parents have expressed a desire for their child to achieve proficiency in both languages. In this program, students are also immersed in instruction that supports their home language and culture. Grade appropriate leveled texts (English/Spanish to support home language) will be used to support discussion. In addition, writing stems/sentence frames will be used to engage students in conversation with family members. Additional supports include, visual aids, read alouds, videos, vocabulary; computer based instructional resources. To develop student's early literacy skills, mCLASS, Heggerty in English and Spanish and OG will be used. To aide students with comprehending grade appropriate skills, the curriculum HMH Into Reading and Arriba la Lectura (HMH Spanish) will be used in the classroom in grades K-5. How does your school ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes are provided according to students’ English language proficiency levels in each program model? In order to ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes is provided according to students’ English language proficiency level in each program model, administrators engage in ongoing yearlong recruitment based on availability of candidates holding proper certification. Hired pedagogues are aligned to class programs accordingly to meet C.R. Part 154.2 mandates based on students’ most current NYSESLAT levels. Administrators and teachers collaborate to ensure that schedules are aligned to meet the mandated units of study in accordance to CR Part 154.2, based on students’ ML/ELLs proficiency levels and program type. According to the CR Part 154.2, students who have scored at the Entering level on the NYSESLAT receive the required 180 minutes of Stand-Alone ENL per week and 180 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA per week. ML/ELLs who have scored at the Emerging level on the NYSESLAT receive 180 minutes of Stand-Alone ENL per week, 180 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA per week. ELLs/MLLs who scored at the Transitioning level on the NYSESLAT receive either 90 minutes of Stand-Alone ENL or 90 minutes of Integrated Content Area in addition to the 90 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA per week. Students who scored at the Expanding level on the NYSESLAT receive 180 minutes per week of ENL/ELA or other content area, these students are not required to receive minutes for Stand-Alone ENL. Students in these classes who scored at the Commanding level on the NYSESLAT receive a minimum of 90 minutes of Integrated ENL/ELA or other content area. These students are not required to receive minutes for Standalone ENL. Students in these classes that scored at the Commanding Level on the NYSESLAT are mandated to receive an additional two years of ENL instruction. Teachers of ML/ELLs in Integrated ENL, Dual Language and Transitional Bilingual Educational programs ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes are provided according to the students' English language proficiency levels by scheduling Integrated and Stand Alone periods in their program. During our Common Planning periods, teachers, coaches and instructional leads analyze the ENL data for students and collaborate to deliver the correct number of instructional minutes. Out-of-classroom ENL providers work closely with classroom teachers to ensure that English Language Learners (ELLs) receive the mandated number of instructional minutes based on their English language proficiency levels. These providers support the scheduling and delivery of both Stand-Alone and Integrated ENL instruction. During Common Planning periods, ENL teachers, coaches, and instructional leads analyze student ENL data and partner with classroom teachers to ensure compliance with instructional time requirements and to support targeted, language-rich instruction. In addition, students who scored at the ENTERING to EXPANDING level on the NYSESLAT and are in a Transitional Bilingual program receive one Home Language Arts (HLA) unit of instruction. Students at the ENTERING and EMERGING levels receive a minimum two Bilingual Content Area subjects and students at the TRANSITIONING and EXPANDING levels receive a minimum of one Bilingual Content Area subject. 3. For schools with Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) programs: a. Which Dual Language Bilingual Education model is implemented? Explain how much of the instructional day (percentage) English proficient students and ELLs are integrated. Our school implements the Two-Way Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) Program, also known as “two-way immersion.” In this model, English Language Learners (ELLs) who share the same home language and English Proficient (EP) students—including native English speakers, former ELLs, and heritage speakers—are integrated for instruction and learn academic content in both English and the partner language. In the Dual Language program, English Dual Language Teachers and ENL Providers collaborate to ensure that schedules are aligned to meet the mandated units of study in accordance to CR Part 154.2, based on students’ ML/ELL proficiency levels and program type. All Dual Language students receive bilingual content area instruction, in accordance with CR Part 154.2. For Dual Language programs, a 50/50 model is implemented. Students receive 50% of the yearly instruction in Spanish and 50% of the yearly instruction in English. Both literacy and content are taught equally in both Spanish and English over the course of the program. Instruction is delivered in one language at a time without translation. Our DL self-contained and DL ICT programs extends from grades K-5. In our Two-Way DLBE classrooms, English proficient students and ELLs are integrated for 100% of the instructional day. Instruction is delivered in both English and the partner language using a balanced model that supports language development and content mastery in both languages. This structure promotes collaboration and interaction among all students while supporting equity, inclusion, and academic excellence. b. In which language(s) is each core content area taught? Core content area instruction is delivered in compliance with the legislative mandates and directives for each ML/ELL program as described in CR Part 154.2. The Transitional Bilingual Education teachers and Dual Language teachers align their instruction according to content units of study and the Next Generation Learning Standards (NGLS). Teachers utilize Language Objectives to provide all students with equal access to the curriculum as well. The teachers employ scaffolding strategies to enrich home language development in addition to English language development in all content areas. HMH Into Reading and Arriba la Lectura materials are utilized to support stages of language and literacy development for our diverse populations. The HMH Into Reading and Arriba la Lectura curricula enable students of varied proficiency levels to increase their reading and writing skills with the implementation of a variety of strategies. To develop Math skills in the home language and the English language, we construct our lessons by bringing the language of Math that our ML/ELLs encounter during their Envisions instruction.
