What is the composition of the ELL subgroups at your school? What percentage of ELLs are Newcomers? How does effective instruction for Newcomers differ from those provided to Developing or Long-Term ELLs? According to the September 2025 RLER data, 78% (665 students) of our English Language Learners are classified as Newcomers, defined as students with 0–3 years of enrollment in NYCDOE schools. This significant proportion shapes our instructional planning and service delivery to ensure both compliance with mandates and the academic and linguistic development of all ELLs. Effective Instruction for Newcomers is a priority at PS 143Q. For Newcomer ELLs, we ensure that they receive the mandated service minutes as outlined in CR Part 154.2. Instruction is then differentiated based on English language proficiency levels. For Entering and Emerging Levels, the focus is on helping students acclimate to their new learning environment while developing both conversational and academic language. Instruction emphasizes Advanced Literacies Hallmark 2: Talk and Discussion, using structured oral language tasks to build foundational communication skills and content knowledge in a low-stress, supportive setting. Students at the Transitioning and Expanding Levels engage in more collaborative, talk-based learning. Teachers design opportunities for student-led discussions, cooperative group work, and project-based tasks during integrated content-area instruction. The goal is to increase both the quantity and quality of student discourse, promoting deeper engagement and language development. Effective instruction for Developing ELLs is also a priority at PS 143Q. For ELLs who have progressed beyond the Newcomer stage and have been classified as Developing ELLs, instruction continues to emphasize meaningful discussion, but with a shift in focus. At this stage, we emphasize Advanced Literacies Hallmark 4: Vocabulary and Knowledge in the Disciplines. Teachers intentionally incorporate a small set of high-utility academic vocabulary words into lessons to help students build both the breadth and depth of content knowledge across subject areas. For Newcomers, instruction relies heavily on visual supports and accessible language to make content understandable. Teachers and peers model language using various sentence structures, and Total Physical Response (TPR) is often used to connect language to meaning. Vocabulary instruction focuses on Tier 1 and Tier 2 words, along with cognates when possible, to build foundational language skills in a meaningful and engaging way. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 151 of 250 In contrast, Developing ELLs engage with more complex language structures, including compound and complex sentences. Vocabulary instruction targets Tier 2 and Tier 3 academic words that deepen content knowledge. Language modeling by teachers and peers remains essential, but students also explore vocabulary through making connections with cognates, morphology, and engaging activities such as games and vocabulary notebooks. This approach supports students’ growing linguistic sophistication and academic language development. For both groups, a student-friendly, NYSESLAT-aligned speaking rubric is used to clearly communicate expectations for oral language development. This tool helps students understand the components of effective spoken language and supports their progress along the linguistic continuum. It also serves as a guide for self-assessment and reflection, promoting ownership of their language learning. By tailoring instruction based on time in the system and proficiency levels, we ensure that all ELL subgroups (Newcomers and Developing ELLs) at PS 143Q receive the targeted support they need to thrive academically and linguistically. (Please note: We do not have any Long-Term ELLs, since we are a K-5 school. What percentage of ELLs are Students with Inconsistent/Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE)? How do you differentiate effective instruction for SIFE? According to the September 2025 RFSF data, PS143Q has 2 identified SIFE students, which represents 0.2% of our ELL population. When a potential SIFE student enrolls, we follow the recommended interview process outlined in C.R. Part 154.2 to determine their SIFE status. Identified students complete the SIFE oral interview questionnaire and the Multilingual Literacy SIFE screener to assess their educational backgrounds and literacy levels. Additionally, all students—regardless of SIFE status—are screened using mCLASS as a universal tool to determine their starting points for Response to Intervention (RTI) and targeted support programs. To meet the unique needs of SIFE students, our school employs multiple strategies to differentiate Instruction and support our students. Teachers create flexible and differentiated small groups that target individual student needs, ensuring personalized instruction. In addition, ENL and RTI teachers collaborate within classrooms to provide direct, targeted assistance for SIFE students alongside their peers. Collaborative teacher teams establish partnerships to promote consistency and scaffolded learning experiences. SIFE students are placed in a bilingual setting. This setting supports cross-linguistic transfers between the home language (Spanish) and the new language (English). It also lowers the affective filter supporting a student's social- emotional well-being, which helps a student feel safe and supported in an academic setting. Another important component of supporting our SIFE students is social-emotional supports. At-risk counseling is provided by our School Counselor, Intervention Specialist, and Parent Coordinator to address barriers to learning and support student well-being. The school administration regularly reviews and adjusts programs to best support SIFE students. This includes thoughtful placement decisions, such as enrollment in our Dual Language program when appropriate, to foster both home language and English development. To accelerate progress, we provide additional instructional opportunities beyond the regular school day, including before- and after-school sessions as well as Saturday classes. SIFE students receive intensified support from TESOL-certified teachers. Instructional materials are utilized to ensure SIFE students make progress. Their progress is assessed, as students are continually monitored with mCLASS to track literacy development. Assessments in the SIFE students’ home language, such as the Spanish LAB, also help inform instruction and growth. Instruction is carefully differentiated based on proficiency levels using leveled benchmark books and curricula such as HMH Into Reading and Arriba la Lectura. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 152 of 250 For bilingual settings, mClass and HMH assessments are administered in Spanish and English. Therefore, students are assessed in both their home and new language. This provides data so staff can strategically support the student's home language, while simultaneously developing English language acquisition. By combining careful identification, flexible instructional models, targeted academic supports, and social-emotional interventions, PS143Q ensures that SIFE students receive the comprehensive and differentiated education necessary to accelerate their academic and language growth. What percentage of ELLs are Long-Term ELLs? How does effective instruction for Long-Term ELLs differ from instruction designed for Developing ELLs? According to the September 2025 RLER data, 0% of our students are currently classified as Long-Term ELLs. Under C.R. Part 154.2, Long-Term ELLs are defined as students who have received English as a New Language instruction —either as part of a bilingual education program or a freestanding ENL program—for seven or more enrolled school years within the NYC DOE. While we currently have no Long-Term ELLs, any students who might enter this classification in the future would be referred to Response to Intervention (RTI) services. RTI supports for Long-Term ELLs typically include remedial reading and language programs such as mCLASS, Orton-Gillingham (OG), Heggerty phonemic awareness, and Spire Decodables to address persistent gaps in literacy and language skills. Developing ELLs, as defined by C.R. Part 154.2, are students who have received ENL instruction for four to six enrolled school years in the NYC DOE. According to the September 2025 RLER data, our school currently has 186 Developing ELLs, which represents 22% of our ELL population. Since PS143Q serves grades K–5, many Developing ELLs may transition into Long-Term ELL status upon entering middle school. To proactively address their needs, we provide mandated ENL service minutes alongside targeted instructional strategies focused on Advanced Literacies Hallmark 3: Writing to Build Language and Knowledge. Writing has been an instructional focus at PS 143Q, because this modality has been identified as the lowest- performing language modality across our school based on historical data and the September 2025 RLAT results. Therefore, to strengthen both language proficiency and academic content knowledge, we emphasize extending and deepening writing protocols aligned with Advanced Literacies Hallmark 3. This targeted approach supports Developing ELLs in building the critical skills necessary to avoid becoming Long-Term ELLs. While we currently have no Long-Term ELLs, we are committed to providing Developing ELLs with intensive, writing- focused instruction to accelerate their language and academic growth, ensuring they continue progressing toward English proficiency and academic success. What percentage of students are former ELLs? How is your school providing mandated instruction to former ELLs? According to our September 2025 RFSF data, 126 students, representing 10% of our total enrollment, are categorized as Former ELLs. According to the CR Part 154, to support Former ELLs in maintaining and further developing their English proficiency, our school provides a minimum of 90 minutes per week of integrated ENL instruction. While we currently have many ENL-certified staff members to ensure that all Former ELLs consistently receive the mandated instructional minutes, DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 153 of 250 we are also actively planning to hire additional ENL-certified staff. Teachers utilize the most recent data from the 2025 NYSESLAT to inform their small group instruction. This data-driven approach helps educators tailor their teaching by selecting appropriate research-based resources and strategies that promote the continued growth and mastery of English language skills for Former ELLs. By providing targeted, data-informed instruction and expanding our staff with additional ENL-certified teachers, we ensure Former ELLs receive the support necessary to sustain and build upon their language proficiency for academic success. Since many of our staff are ENL certified, we are able to utilize Option D: Integrated English as a New Language (ENL) program. During ENL instruction, content area instruction is delivered in English. This instruction is supplemented with home language supports, such as usage of cognates, and other scaffolds, such as providing complex sentence structures. For our former ELLs, ENL instruction is delivered using the Integrated model of instruction. 2. Examine all at-risk levels that might adversely affect ELLs at your school. What trends do you notice about the at-risk levels of ELLs at your school? According to the Spring 2025 EDAT data, 27.6% (254 students) of our ELL students have one at-risk indicator, while 35.7% (328 students) have two at-risk indicators. Notably, 100% of our ELLs have at least one at-risk factor, with no students having more than seven indicators. The largest group, representing 35.7% (328 students) of our ELLs, have at least two risk factors. We have noticed some At-Risk Trends and Indicators for certain subgroups. The Newcomers subgroup is the majority of our ELL population with a total of 745 students (81%). This reflects the challenges associated with recent enrollment and language acquisition. Students in Temporary Housing is a significant portion of our student population with 350 students (38%). ELLs experiencing temporary housing adds an additional layer of instability which affects academic progress. Another subgroup where we have noticed an at-risk trend is students whose data shows that 409 (44.5%) of our ELLs have been categorized as not meeting expected progress. This signals the need for targeted intervention. We have identified other At-Risk factors. Additional indicators impacting our ELLs include being identified as a potential Long-Term ELL, and/or scoring a level 1 or 2 in English Language Arts and/or Math for one or two consecutive years. Our data reveals that the majority of our ELL students face multiple risk factors, with Newcomer status and housing instability being the most prevalent. These trends highlight the critical need for comprehensive academic and social supports tailored to the unique challenges faced by our ELL population. To address at-risk trends—such as newcomer status, temporary housing, and limited ELP progress—we provide specific instructional and social-emotional supports. In terms of social-emotional supports, we host February and April Break camps for students in temporary housing. Throughout the week at camp, students engage in fun activities and have an additional opportunity to participate in small group activities. Since it is a small group setting, the teachers and other support staff have the opportunity to discuss the students feelings and further address their well-being. We also host newcomer activities, such as Paint and Sip, where students create a paining under the guidance of an artist, and can socialize with other Newcomers and their families. In addition, we host a Saturday Academy and a Sunday Academy to focus on the needs of our at-risk populations for both social-emotional development and making progress in English language proficiency. Additionally, we have our own after school program called Happy Corona, which is open to all students. During this after school program, staff provides homework help and small group instruction. Students participate in fun activities that support social-emotional well- being and English language acquisition. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 154 of 250 3. Examine all at-risk levels that might adversely affect former ELLs at your school. What trends do you notice about the at-risk levels of former ELLs at your school? According to the Spring 2025 EDAT data, 30 Former ELLs (39.5%) at our school have at least one at-risk indicator. No Former ELLs currently have more than four at-risk indicators. We have identified trends among Former ELLs. An analysis of the data reveals the following key trends impacting Former ELLs. In terms of the former ELLs academic performance, 28 students (36.8%) scored a level 1 or 2 on the ELA State Test during the previous school year. In addition, 18 students (23.7%) scored a level 1 or 2 on the Math State Test, indicating continued academic needs in core content areas. Another at-risk indicator is housing instability. 19 students (25%) are currently living in temporary housing, which can negatively impact academic consistency and access to learning resources. Nearly 64.5% of our Former ELLs exhibit at-risk indicators, with academic underperformance and housing instability being the most prevalent factors. These findings highlight the importance of continued monitoring and targeted support for Former ELLs to ensure sustained academic progress and overall stability following their transition out of ENL services. These trends are monitored and used to inform planning for Former ELLs. Our school closely monitors these trends through regular data reviews, including EDAT reporting cycles, academic performance tracking, and coordination with support staff such as guidance counselors, ENL Teachers and the Students in Temporary Housing Coordinator. This ongoing monitoring allows us to identify patterns and respond proactively. For instance, former ELLs who show academic or housing-related risk factors are flagged for additional interventions such as targeted small-group instruction, academic tutoring, check-ins with social workers, and referrals to external support services. The data-driven insights gained from our trend analysis are used to design instructional planning, allocate resources, and tailor support services to address the specific needs of former ELLs. By continuously reviewing and acting on these indicators, we aim to ensure that former ELLs maintain academic growth and stability after transitioning out of ENL services. 4. What are the home languages of ELLs with the largest representation at your school? What staff, structures, and processes does your school have to support communication and learning that values these home languages? According to the June 2025 RFSF data, the predominant home language among English Language Learners (ELLs) at PS143Q is Spanish, with 905 students (98.4%) identifying Spanish as their primary language. In addition to Spanish, our ELL population includes students who speak: Chinese/Mandarin (9 students), Russian (2 students), Macedonian (1 student), Mandinka (1 student), Nepali (1 student), and Bengali (1 student). Staffing and Communication Supports are important to ensure families are welcomed and well-informed. PS 143Q prioritizes hiring bilingual staff across various roles, including: Parent Coordinator, Family Workers, School Aides, Paraprofessionals, and Instructional and Pedagogical Staff. These team members play a vital role in bridging communication between the school and families, especially those who speak Spanish and Chinese, the two most commonly spoken home languages. All school notices and announcements are translated into Spanish and Chinese. For families who speak less common languages, teachers utilize the NYCDOE Translation and Interpretation Line to ensure that communication remains clear, inclusive, and respectful of every family's linguistic background. To support learning in students’ home languages, PS143Q offers academic supports, in addition to bilingual classes at every grade level, allowing Spanish-speaking students to access rigorous content while continuing to develop literacy and academic skills in both English and Spanish. This dual-language approach affirms students’ cultural DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 155 of 250 identities and promotes biliteracy. All staff is required to completed the Language Access Training, which will equip staff with essential tools to better support families who prefer to communicate in a language other than English. In addition, staff participates in professional development about cross-linguistic transfers. Staff learns what this means and strategies they can use to leverage what students know in their home language to teach a new language (English). In addition, bilingual sound walls are utilized across various grade levels, so that staff can hone in on various aspects of the home language and new language to make meaningful connections. As for assessments for mClass, HMH, and envisions (math program) in bilingual classes, the assessments are provided using the home language strategically and/or side-by-side for English language development. Through intentional staffing, translation services, and robust bilingual programming, PS143Q ensures that students' home languages are recognized, respected, and integrated into both learning and communication practices. 5. What are the racial and ethnic classifications of ELLs at your school? How are ELLs being included in planning and delivery of Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CR-SE)? According to the Spring 2025 EDAT data, 901 (97.9%) of ELLs at our school identify as Hispanic or Latinx, 1.3% as Asian, 0.2% as Black, and 0.5% as White. To ensure a welcoming and affirming environment for all ELLs, we prioritize inclusive communication, curriculum, and materials that reflect and honor students' diverse cultures and communities. Our school supports project-based learning that supports and uplifts our community. Each grade level has a STEM class, in which our ELLs are empowered to develop language and content through a STEM curriculum. This work includes a student leadership component to amplify student voices through the presentation of STEM projects in the school-wide STEM fair. Grade Teams collaborate to ensure curriculum and assessments are inclusive, culturally responsive, and tailored to diverse learning styles, while also integrating current events. Our school implements the HMH Into Reading curriculum, which represents characters from diverse backgrounds. Instructional resources are developed with the needs of our students in mind, ensuring we are providing linguistic support so that student voices can be amplified during discussions bringing in various racially, culturally, and linguistically diverse perspectives. ELLs are actively included in CR-SE planning and delivery through various leadership and participatory roles. For example, one of the projects we worked on was through the NYC DOE Citywide Project- Based Learning Initiatives. Multilingual student ambassadors participated in representing our school in the District 24 PBL culminating presentation. We also have equitable opportunities for school-wide leadership. Through these opportunities, students are able to provide input on school culture, climate, and culturally responsive initiatives. Student leaders co-facilitate peer orientation sessions for newly arrived ELLs, offering both linguistic and social-emotional support. We also have students that participate in Pawprints, which is our in-school civics magazine. They write articles about school events and various community events, such as projects to get additional books for our school, ending bullying, and stopping littering. Additionally, students participate in our school's Lion News Broadcast, where they have an opportunity to share and showcase different projects students in our school are working on. Additionally, we are committed to ongoing professional learning, including diversity, equity, and inclusion training for staff. This year, all parent-facing staff are completing the Language Access training. Families will also be engaged and supported through various workshops and meetings hosted by our ENL team, STH Coordinator, Counselors, and PTA. As a result, families feel welcomed and encouraged to come to our school and reach out to staff for assistance with various needs, such as supporting their child or obtaining information about community resources. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 156 of 250 6. What trends do you notice in reviewing English Language Proficiency (ELP) growth at your school? How many students met ELP sufficient progress? How many students did not meet ELP sufficient progress? Based on the Spring 2025 EDAT data, a total of 556 students were identified as Progress Eligible for English Language Proficiency (ELP) growth. Of these, 147 students—approximately 26.4%—met their ELP growth targets through a variety of methods. Specifically, 76 students (13.7%) met their annual progress targets through Method 2, 41 students (7.4%) exited ELL status by meeting proficiency through Method 1, and 30 students (5.4%) made progress through Method 3 by meeting Safe Harbor cumulative growth. However, 409 students (73.6%) did not meet sufficient progress, highlighting the need for continued and targeted instructional support to improve overall language proficiency outcomes. In response to this trend, our school implemented a series of structured, data-informed common planning sessions across grade levels. These sessions focused on deepening staff understanding of the ELL identification process, the administration and structure of the NYSITELL and NYSESLAT exams, and the interpretation of key data sets, including RLAT and FURBY reports. Educators were guided through a detailed review of each section of the NYSESLAT exam by grade or grade-band, with particular emphasis on analyzing student performance across modalities—listening, speaking, reading, and writing. This analysis allowed teachers to identify individual student strengths and areas in need of support, enabling them to use stronger modalities to reinforce weaker ones. Additionally, FURBY data was used to track student progress or regression within specific modalities, offering actionable insights that inform instruction and intervention. Teachers applied this data to tailor lesson plans, incorporate targeted skill-building activities, and create dynamic small group instruction based on students' specific linguistic needs. For example, if a class demonstrated a need for growth in listening comprehension, teachers integrated more listening-focused tasks into daily instruction. This strategic, data-driven approach ensures that instructional planning is both responsive and intentional, aimed at supporting ELLs in making measurable progress toward the next proficiency level on the NYSESLAT and ultimately achieving English language proficiency. 7. When you look at the results of the New York State English as a Second Language Achievement Test (NYSESLAT), what stands out? What trends are there for each modality? An analysis of the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT data for both current and former English Language Learners (ELLs) reveals notable trends across the four language modalities: Listening, Speaking, Reading, and Writing. One of the most significant findings is that Speaking emerges as the greatest area of need, particularly among students in the earlier grade levels. Writing also presents challenges, especially in the upper grades, suggesting that while foundational writing skills may begin to develop in early elementary, students require continued, sustained support to achieve proficiency as academic demands increase. In contrast, Reading shows moderate levels of need across grade levels, and Listening consistently stands out as the modality in which students demonstrate the strongest performance. These patterns have informed our instructional planning and support structures. For students struggling with Speaking, we incorporate student-friendly rubrics to help model strong verbal responses and provide targeted scaffolds based on proficiency levels. Entering and Emerging ELLs receive sentence starters to initiate and structure their oral responses, while Transitioning and Expanding ELLs benefit from sentence structures designed to deepen and refine their spoken English. These same linguistic supports are also used to strengthen writing development, particularly in early grades, where students are beginning to organize thoughts and express them in written form. For students at more advanced proficiency levels, we introduce structured paragraph writing strategies to help them construct cohesive, grade-level appropriate responses. Instructional decisions are guided by ongoing analysis of student data during our Common Planning sessions, where DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 157 of 250 teachers identify trends by modality and proficiency level. This data-driven approach allows educators to design targeted interventions, build on students' strengths, and address specific areas of need. Listening, consistently the most accessible modality, serves as a foundation upon which other language skills can be strengthened. Meanwhile, the gradual improvement in Speaking and Writing—especially by Grades 4 and 5—demonstrates the effectiveness of intentional scaffolding and sustained language development supports over time. 8. What is the relationship between students’ performance on the NYSESLAT and other state assessments such as the ELA assessment, the Math assessment, and the Regents exams? According to the Spring 2025 EDAT data, there is a clear and significant relationship between students’ English language proficiency, as measured by the NYSESLAT, and their performance on other state assessments such as the ELA and Math exams. In particular, students at the Entering and Emerging levels of English proficiency tend to score lower on the ELA exam. For example, 31% of students who scored at Level 1 on the ELA also scored at the Entering or Emerging levels on the NYSESLAT, with only a small number—just four students—scoring at the Commanding level. Among students who scored Level 2 on the ELA, 19% were at the Expanding or Commanding levels, while 100% of students who scored Level 3 on the ELA were also at the Commanding level on the NYSESLAT. These findings suggest that students’ English language development directly impacts their ability to demonstrate literacy skills on the ELA exam, which requires a high level of comprehension, academic vocabulary, and written expression. A similar trend is observed in the Math assessment. While math is generally less language-dependent than ELA, students with higher English proficiency still tend to perform better. For example, 10% of students who scored Level 2 in Math were at the Entering or Emerging levels, while 24.3% were at the Transitioning or Expanding levels, and only 2.9% were Commanding. Among students who scored Level 3 in Math, the majority were at the Transitioning, Expanding, or Commanding levels, with only 1.3% at the Entering or Emerging levels. Notably, a few students at the Expanding and Commanding levels achieved a Level 4 in Math. This indicates that as English proficiency increases, students are better able to comprehend and respond to the language used in math questions, particularly when required to explain their reasoning or interpret multi-step problems. These patterns highlight the importance of integrating language development into content instruction. Teachers use targeted strategies such as vocabulary instruction, sentence structures, and reading techniques to help students understand and respond to exam questions across subjects. For instance, students are taught how to identify key words and phrases in math problems to determine the correct operation, and they use structured language to explain their mathematical thinking. Similarly, in ELA, instruction is differentiated by proficiency level, with Entering and Emerging students learning to use simple sentences, while Transitioning and Expanding students are supported in using more complex structures. Overall, these insights inform curriculum planning and instructional design across disciplines. They highlight the necessity of supporting English learners not only with content knowledge, but also with continued, strategic language development to ensure that students can fully demonstrate their academic abilities in all tested subjects. 9. What are trends reflected in the NYSESLAT results of ELL subgroups such as ELLs with IEPs and SIFE? What do you think is impacting the results for these subgroups? Our school currently serves two students with Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE) and 221 English Language Learners (ELLs) with Individualized Education Programs (IEPs), representing 26% of our total ELL population. According to the September 2025 RFSF data, proficiency levels among ELLs with IEPs show that 18.1% are performing at the Entering level, 28.5% at Emerging, 35.3% at Transitioning, 18.1% at Expanding, and only 6.3% have reached the Commanding level. Analysis of the 2025 Spring NYSESLAT results reveals a continuing trend of underperformance in the writing modality within this subgroup. Writing remains a significant challenge due to the dual demands these students face—developing English language proficiency while simultaneously navigating learning challenges that impact cognitive and linguistic processing. Many ELLs with IEPs struggle with retaining, DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 158 of 250 organizing, and expressing language, particularly in complex academic tasks that require advanced written and oral communication skills. To address these challenges, our school is actively working to close instructional gaps between English as a New Language (ENL) and special education services. We are implementing Orton-Gillingham-based literacy interventions and utilizing mCLASS assessments to inform targeted small-group instruction. A key strategy involves fostering collaboration among ENL teachers, special education providers, and general education staff. The school’s Instructional Leadership Team plays a central role in promoting a rigorous, supportive, and inclusive learning environment for all students. Regular meetings are held with special education teachers and paraprofessionals to review best practices and ensure consistent implementation of IEP supports. These strategies are also integrated into common planning sessions across and within grade levels, strengthening collaboration and shared ownership of student success. For our SIFE students, NYSESLAT performance is notably affected by limited or interrupted formal education. These students often face substantial academic gaps and underdeveloped literacy skills in their home language, making the acquisition of academic English particularly difficult. Furthermore, limited prior exposure to structured classroom environments can pose additional barriers to engagement and learning. In response, our school has implemented targeted academic interventions, increased access to language-rich instruction, and enhanced social-emotional supports to aid in students' acclimation and academic progress. SIFE students are strategically placed in bilingual programs to support second language development while leveraging their home language. Interventions such as Orton-Gillingham-based literacy instruction, mCLASS assessments in both English and Spanish, and the use of bilingual vocabulary across subjects are employed to accelerate learning. Upon registration, a dedicated support team is assembled to address the students' academic and social-emotional needs. Additionally, SIFE students are invited to participate in Winter and Spring Break academic camps, providing further opportunities to support their academic growth and language development. 10. Tier 1 Core Instruction 1. How does your school provide ELLs with a high-quality academic experience that leads to deeper learning? Our school is committed to providing English Language Learners (ELLs) with a high-quality academic experience that promotes deeper learning and long-term success. The Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) plays a central role in cultivating a supportive learning environment and setting high expectations for all students. To ensure ELLs are both challenged and supported, the ILT engages in ongoing professional development and actively recruits qualified educators who hold the appropriate certifications to teach multilingual learners (MLs)/ELLs. Collaboration between administrators and teachers is essential in designing instruction that is responsive to students’ English proficiency levels and program models. Instructional time is thoughtfully planned and tailored to meet the specific needs of ELLs. The ILT regularly reviews and refines the curriculum to align with the linguistic and academic needs of these students, ensuring that it reflects the four hallmarks of strong core instruction: engagement, rigor, targeted support, and meaningful assessment. Instruction is grounded in research-based practices that promote language development alongside content mastery. In addition, instructional coaches play a vital role in supporting this work by providing ongoing professional development focused on core content areas, analyzing student data, and guiding staff in using strategies that effectively support ELL growth. These sessions help educators deepen their understanding of how to differentiate instruction, scaffold learning, and use data to drive instruction specifically tailored to ELLs’ language development and academic progress. Through continuous monitoring, coaching, and strategic adjustments, the ILT ensures that ELLs receive the personalized support necessary to thrive. These comprehensive and intentional efforts create an academic environment where ELLs can access deeper learning opportunities and reach their full potential. 2. Long Term ELLs To ensure that Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs) have meaningful access to grade- level academic content and to accelerate their English language development, our school implements a range of research-based instructional strategies, materials, and technologies tailored to meet the specific needs of various ELL subgroups. While we currently do not have any officially identified Long-Term ELLs, we actively monitor students who are at risk of becoming Long-Term ELLs, using data from the September 2025 RLER report. All identified MLs and ELLs are assessed using mCLASS as a universal screener to determine entry points for intensive groups during our WIN period, these groups focus on the students’ areas of need, utilizing their areas of strength to further develop their English proficiency. Students who need further support will be identified and supported through Response to Intervention (RTI) and targeted support programs. For students at risk of long-term ELL status, instruction is designed to be both rigorous and supportive, with a strong emphasis on Tier II and Tier III academic vocabulary. These students engage in close reading of complex texts to build deeper comprehension and strengthen critical thinking skills. ENL and bilingual teachers play a central role in planning and delivering differentiated instruction that supports language development within the context of core content areas. Instructional strategies include explicit vocabulary instruction, the use of multi-leveled texts, visual supports, videos, and interactive technology tools that provide accessible entry points into grade-level content. ENL educators work in close collaboration with classroom teachers to co-plan and share effective instructional practices. This ensures that language development is embedded throughout the curriculum and that instruction remains culturally and linguistically responsive. To support students at risk of becoming Long-Term ELLs, we regularly monitor their progress and adjust instruction as needed. During Common Planning sessions, classroom teachers and members of the instructional support team analyze ENL data using the RLAT and FURBY reports. This process involves evaluating whether students made progress from the previous year, identifying strengths and areas for growth across each modality, and determining which students require additional support to advance their English language proficiency. In addition to these reports, teachers rely on observations, mCLASS data, HMH module assessments, and enVisions topic assessments to monitor student progress and adjust instruction accordingly. To further support these students, we offer a variety of extended learning opportunities, including literacy-based morning programs, Title III enrichment, and Saturday Academy. These programs are designed to reinforce academic language, increase exposure to complex texts, and provide additional instructional support in a small-group setting. In alignment with CR Part 154.2, our school ensures that the academic and linguistic needs of MLs and ELLs are addressed through consistent collaboration between ENL and classroom teachers, differentiated instruction and materials, and intentional, data-informed planning. This comprehensive approach helps prevent students from falling behind and promotes steady progress in both content mastery and English language development. 5. What supports does your school provide to ELLs with IEPs to achieve their IEP goals and attain English language proficiency within the least restrictive environment? In order to ensure that Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) receive the mandated number of instructional minutes aligned with their English language proficiency levels and program models, the school’s Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) engages in ongoing professional development and year-round recruitment of certified staff. Once hired, teachers are aligned to class programs based on students’ most current NYSESLAT levels to ensure compliance and effective service delivery. Additionally, the ILT meets regularly to review and select curricula that offer multiple entry points and are designed to address the diverse linguistic and academic needs of MLs/ELLs. For students who are identified as ELLs and having Individualized Education Programs (IEPs), teachers ensure placement in the least restrictive environment based on each student’s specific learning needs and goals. Teachers also work in partnership with speech and language professionals to integrate evidence-based strategies that can be applied in inclusive classroom settings. Academic interventions are carefully selected and implemented to match each student’s unique learning profile and IEP objectives, ensuring comprehensive and personalized support. 6. How is home language assessed in each program model (DLBE, TBE, and ENL)? Home language assessment is tailored in each program model—Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE), Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE), and English as a New Language (ENL)—to meet the distinct linguistic and academic needs of Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs). In the ENL program, home language assessment primarily serves to inform instructional planning. For Spanish- speaking students, the Spanish Language Assessment Battery-Revised (LAB-R) is administered to determine language dominance and guide appropriate services throughout the year. ENL teachers also leverage computer- based programs in the HMH platform and use home-language instructional materials from curricula such as HMH and Envisions to support students in building English proficiency while valuing their first language. In the Dual Language (DLBE) program, students’ literacy skills in their home language are assessed through both formative and summative measures. The Spanish version of mCLASS (Lecturas) is used to monitor early literacy development in Spanish. The DLBE model follows a 50/50 alternating-day approach, with equal instructional time in English and Spanish. Students benefit from access to home language libraries, bilingual textbooks, and digital tools like HMH computer-based programs and Raz-Kids, which are integrated into instruction. Scaffolded instruction and small group work help reinforce skills in both languages, supporting a balanced development of biliteracy. In the Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program, home language assessment is also conducted using mCLASS (Lecturas) in Spanish. For students at the Entering and Emerging levels, instruction begins with a 70/30 split—70% in Spanish and 30% in English—and gradually shifts toward more English as students develop greater proficiency. Home language development is supported through biliteracy resources such as HMH Into Reading, Arriba la Lectura, Envisions, and supplemental technology programs including HMH computer-based programs and Raz-Kids. These resources are aligned to promote both language and content mastery. Across all three program models, teachers, instructional coaches, and administrators use data from home language assessments to inform instruction, provide targeted supports, and plan differentiated, scaffolded lessons that bridge home language skills with English development. This comprehensive, data-informed approach honors students’ linguistic backgrounds while accelerating academic growth and English proficiency. Describe systems and structures for supporting co-teaching and collaboration among teachers for all ELL program models available at your school (e.g. integrated co-teaching, Dual Language Bilingual Education teacher partnerships, etc.). The following systems and structures for supporting co-teaching and collaboration among teachers for all ELL program models are available at our school. Targeted Professional Development: In addition to general professional learning sessions, our school offers co- teaching workshops that focus specifically on effective co-teaching models, such as parallel teaching, station teaching, and team teaching. These sessions emphasize how ENL teachers and general education teachers can collaborate seamlessly in the classroom, sharing responsibilities for both content delivery and language development. For instance, during station teaching, the class may be divided into small groups, with each teacher leading a station to reinforce different aspects of content and language skills. Coaching: Our school provides ongoing coaching where instructional coaches or experienced ENL teachers observe co-taught classrooms and offer real-time feedback. This support helps teachers refine their co-teaching practices, ensuring that they are implementing the strategies learned during professional development in their day-to-day instruction. Coaches may also assist in the co-planning process, helping to align content objectives with language goals and ensuring differentiation for students with varying English proficiency levels. Common Planning Time: A key system in place is the common planning sessions that are scheduled across all grade levels. These structured sessions give ENL teachers and content teachers dedicated time to plan lessons together, discuss individual student needs, and ensure alignment between content and language instruction. During these meetings, teachers work together to develop lesson plans that incorporate both academic content and targeted language objectives, ensuring that lessons are accessible to ELLs and that language development is embedded into everyday instruction. Data-Driven Instructional Planning: Grade meetings and data dives are a cornerstone of our collaborative practice. During these sessions, teachers not only analyze English language proficiency levels but also review student performance on classroom assessments, standardized tests, and language assessments such as the NYSESLAT. By examining this data, teachers can identify trends in student progress, areas of need, and adjust their co-teaching strategies accordingly. For example, if data reveals that a group of students is struggling with academic vocabulary, teachers may incorporate more explicit vocabulary instruction into content lessons. Shared Resources and Instructional Materials: Teachers have access to bilingual instructional materials, language scaffolds, and technology-based programs like translation tools, visual dictionaries, and language learning apps (e.g., Imagine Learning, Raz-Kids). These resources support differentiated instruction within co-taught classrooms, ensuring that both teachers have the tools needed to provide individualized support. Additionally, teachers often collaborate to create shared lesson banks and resource folders where they upload materials that align with both the content curriculum and language development goals. Use of Google Classroom: This platform supports co-teaching by allowing both the ENL and content teachers to share responsibility for posting assignments, providing feedback, and monitoring student progress. This system ensures that students have continuous access to instructional content, including language supports like sentence frames or vocabulary lists, and allows both teachers to monitor student engagement and performance. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 167 of 250 Collaborative Teaching Structures: Co-teaching teams at our school embrace the philosophy of shared responsibility, where both the ENL and content teacher are equally engaged in planning, instruction, and assessment. To reinforce this shared responsibility, teachers co-develop assessments that measure both content mastery and language acquisition. For example, during a science unit, students may be assessed on their understanding of scientific concepts as well as their ability to use content-specific language in their explanations. Interdisciplinary Collaboration: Co-teaching and collaboration extend beyond just ENL and content teachers. At our school, ENL teachers often collaborate with special education teachers and other support staff to ensure that ELLs with diverse needs receive holistic support. This multi-disciplinary approach ensures that all aspects of a student’s learning are considered, from language development to content mastery and even social-emotional learning, contributing to a well-rounded educational experience. 9. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 169 of 250 Assessment SCREENERS 11. Which screening assessments (e.g.iReady, MAP Growth, Acadience, or STAR Reading) does your school use to guide instructional planning for your ELLs? At our school, we utilize MAP Growth and mCLASS assessments for students in grades K-5 to guide instructional planning for Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs). These screeners provide critical data that inform the formation of small instructional groups both within the classroom and for students eligible for Response to Intervention (RTI) or Academic Intervention Services (AIS). ML/ELLs who score below grade level on these assessments receive targeted Tier I supports in their classrooms, as well as additional instruction tailored to their ENL proficiency levels by their ENL teachers. Literacy programs such as mCLASS, Orton-Gillingham (OG), and Heggerty are employed to strengthen foundational skills for students struggling with grade-level content. Conversely, students performing on grade level benefit from small group guided reading lessons designed to support continued growth toward end-of-year reading goals. For ML/ELLs who score above grade level, enrichment opportunities are provided, often involving more independent assignments that target the language modalities that need the most support to foster ongoing progress toward NYSESLAT proficiency. During regular Data Analysis Meetings, the team utilizes the ELL Data Analysis Tool to identify student needs and determine appropriate interventions that promote rigorous and equitable learning experiences. ENL providers collaborate closely with classroom teachers in common planning sessions to examine instructional practices, discuss student progress, and refine teaching strategies. This collaboration focuses on understanding what instruction each student receives, the methods of delivery, the rationale behind instructional choices, and how practices can be improved to better support student outcomes. Additionally, staff participate in professional development sessions aimed at deepening their skills in data analysis and enhancing competencies that support students’ academic and linguistic growth. ENL teachers share data findings and effective strategies during these meetings to strengthen language acquisition and content learning. Furthermore, inquiry analysis teams analyze data to create targeted focus groups, providing additional tailored support to meet student needs. 12. For all grades, list and describe your targeted intervention programs for ELLs in ELA, Math, and other core content areas (specify ELL subgroups targeted). Include the language(s) in which the intervention services are offered. Our school’s targeted intervention programs for ELLs in English Language Arts (ELA), Math, and other core content areas are carefully designed to meet the diverse linguistic and academic needs of students across different proficiency levels and subgroups. In English Language Arts (ELA), interventions for Newcomer and Developing ELLs focus on building foundational reading and writing skills. Programs such as mCLASS, Orton-Gillingham (OG), and Heggerty are used to develop phonemic awareness, decoding, and comprehension. Classroom strategies are visually reinforced to support student understanding and engagement. For Developing ELLs, there is a strong emphasis on academic language DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 170 of 250 development through techniques such as sentence deconstruction and reconstruction, chunking information, and the use of graphic organizers to scaffold complex texts. Bilingual dictionaries and bilingual books provide critical support, facilitating access to content in students’ native language—primarily Spanish—while promoting English language acquisition. Writing instruction is structured to guide students from note-taking to crafting full paragraphs, fostering growth in both conversational and academic English. In Math, our intervention program incorporates Successmaker, an adaptive and personalized learning platform that tailors instruction to each student’s proficiency level. For Newcomer and Developing ELLs, math instruction includes language supports such as text modification, chunking, and the use of visuals to clarify math word problems and concepts. Bilingual resources and paraprofessional support further enhance comprehension by allowing students to approach mathematical content in both English and Spanish. This dual-language approach helps bridge linguistic gaps while building conceptual understanding. Across content areas, our intervention services are primarily delivered in English and Spanish to meet the needs of our ELL population, ensuring equitable access to rigorous instruction while honoring students’ home languages. The instructional focus includes academic vocabulary specific to math, enabling ELLs to engage with key terms in both languages. Strategies like sequencing events and problem-solving scaffolds help students navigate more complex math tasks. In Science and Social Studies, Newcomer and Developing ELLs engage with multi-level text sets that are centered around big ideas and rich content to deepen their understanding of academic concepts. For Entering/Emerging students, Hallmark #1—working with engaging texts—helps develop language skills through high-interest topics. For Transitioning/Expanding students, Hallmark #4—studying high-utility vocabulary—fosters a broader and deeper understanding of content-specific language in both Science and Social Studies. Instructional strategies include the use of videos, vocabulary instruction, and multi-leveled texts to ensure students can access core concepts while building both English and Spanish language skills. Collaborative efforts between the RTI teacher and content-area teachers ensure that linguistic scaffolding is provided, helping ELLs excel academically across the curriculum. The school also offers AIS (Academic Intervention Services) and a WIN (What I Need) period that target specific academic needs for ELLs. These periods provide focused, individualized instruction to address gaps in understanding, offering additional time for language development and content mastery in both English and Spanish. The RTI teacher collaborates with the classroom and ENL teachers to tailor these sessions to each student's unique needs, ensuring a comprehensive support system for their academic and linguistic growth. 13. For all grades, describe how your school uses data to guide instruction for ELLs within a Multi- Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). Refer to the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs and ELLs and MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs to help in the development and implementation of your school’s plan for MLs/ELLs. At our school, data-driven decision-making is at the core of how we support Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) across all grade levels through a Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS), ensuring that instruction is responsive, targeted, and aligned with both the Instructional Leadership Tool and the MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs. Holistic Data Collection: At our school, we take a comprehensive approach to gathering data on ELLs across various domains. In addition to traditional academic assessments like the NYSESLAT and mCLASS, we gather data on a student’s language development history (e.g., number of years in a bilingual or ENL program) and home language DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 171 of 250 literacy levels. This data helps in identifying students’ needs at different tiers of support, particularly for students with inconsistent or interrupted formal education (SIFE) and potential long-term ELLs. By reviewing data in multiple contexts, we ensure that both academic and socio-emotional needs are met. Tier 1: High-quality classroom instruction for all students – In the first tier of MTSS, core instruction is provided to all students, including ELLs, in alignment with CR Part 154.2 mandates. Instruction in Tier 1 is differentiated based on students' English proficiency levels (as measured by NYSESLAT or NYSITELL), home language skills, and other diagnostic tools. For example, students in Tier 1 may receive instruction that includes language scaffolds, such as visuals, sentence frames, and structured peer discussions, to ensure they can access grade-level content. Data from formative assessments and progress monitoring tools, such as mCLASS, informs ongoing adjustments to Tier 1 instruction to ensure it meets the diverse needs of ELLs. Tier 2: Targeted small-group interventions– For students who require additional support beyond core instruction, Tier 2 interventions are implemented. These students are identified through progress monitoring data and assessments of both English and home language literacy. Tier 2 interventions may include small group instruction that focuses on specific language skills, such as phonics, fluency, vocabulary development, or reading comprehension. For example, if data shows that a group of students is struggling with comprehension, they might receive small group instruction with additional support in understanding academic language or key concepts in both English and their home language. Tier 3: Intensive, individualized support– Students who do not make adequate progress in Tier 2 move to Tier 3, where they receive intensive, individualized instruction. The instruction here is more frequent and focused on the specific needs identified through continuous progress monitoring. For example, an ELL with significant challenges in both home language and English literacy might receive one-on-one instruction using a bilingual instructional approach. The school also considers socio-emotional factors and works closely with counselors and other support staff to address any additional needs that might be affecting the student’s academic performance. Use of Progress Monitoring Tools: Tools like mCLASS (K-5 Literacy) allow teachers to track students' growth in real- time across multiple literacy domains, such as phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension. The data collected from beginning-of-year (BOY), middle-of-year (MOY), and end-of-year (EOY) benchmarks, as well as bi-weekly progress monitoring, is disaggregated by ELL status, language proficiency levels, and home language literacy levels. This disaggregated data enables teachers to compare individual student progress against both classroom peers and broader benchmarks, allowing for more targeted instruction. Formative and Summative Assessments: In addition to mCLASS, other assessments (e.g., classroom-based formative assessments, bilingual evaluations, and work samples in both English and the home language) are used to guide instruction. These assessments help teachers identify gaps in both content knowledge and language skills. For example, if a student’s writing samples in both English and their home language show challenges with narrative structure, teachers might focus on strengthening writing through targeted interventions in Tier 2 or 3. Ongoing Data Cycles: Data is analyzed in 6-8 week cycles to ensure continuous monitoring of ELL progress. During these cycles, teams of teachers, including ENL and general education teachers, review multiple data points to make informed decisions about which students require more support and what interventions will be most effective. These cycles include data dives where teachers collaborate to review progress monitoring data, adjust instruction, and set goals for individual students or groups of students. Parent Communication: In accordance with MTSS best practices and CR Part 154.2, our school places a strong emphasis on keeping parents informed and involved in the process. Parents receive regular updates on their child's progress in both English and their home language. These updates include detailed information about the instructional strategies and interventions used, the staff members delivering the support, and the academic or behavioral goals set for their child. For example, if a student moves from Tier 1 to Tier 2, the school will explain the rationale for the additional support and outline how progress will be monitored. Meetings with parents, interpreters, DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 172 of 250 and bilingual staff help ensure clear communication, and families are invited to participate in decision-making about their child’s education plan. Multi-Disciplinary Teams: As part of the MTSS structure, our school uses multi-disciplinary teams that include ENL teachers, general education teachers, counselors, and other support staff. These teams meet regularly to review student data and provide input on interventions. For example, if a student is suspected of having a learning disability, the team conducts a bilingual educational evaluation to determine whether the student’s academic difficulties are language-based or related to another underlying issue. This ensures that students receive the appropriate level of support and are not misidentified as needing special education services when the issue is primarily linguistic. Data-Driven Leadership: Our school’s Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) plays a pivotal role in overseeing the implementation of the MTSS framework for ELLs. The ILT uses the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs/ELLs to guide decision-making, ensuring that data is used strategically to inform instructional practices. For example, if a data review shows that a particular grade level is struggling with academic vocabulary, the ILT might initiate professional development sessions focused on vocabulary acquisition strategies for ELLs, ensuring that all teachers are equipped with effective tools to address the issue. FORMATIVE 14. Which formative assessments (e.g. NYC performance Tasks, ELL Periodic Assessment, HLA Assessment, DRA, teacher-created assessments, etc.) are used to inform and drive instruction? Our school utilizes a variety of formative assessments to inform and drive instruction for Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs), ensuring that instruction is targeted and responsive to student needs across grade levels. The mCLASS assessment is administered to all students in grades K–5 to assess foundational literacy skills. This tool provides valuable data on students’ accuracy, fluency, and comprehension, giving both classroom and ENL teachers clear insight into the literacy development of ELLs. The data collected from mCLASS directly informs small group instruction and helps ensure that students are provided with the appropriate scaffolds and supports to access increasingly complex literacy tasks. To support English language development, particularly in speaking and writing, teachers use student-friendly rubrics aligned with the 2024 NYSESLAT standards. These rubrics, along with end-of-module assessments from the HMH Into Reading curriculum and portfolio-based writing tasks, allow students and teachers to identify areas of strength and areas for growth. This formative feedback supports progress toward English language proficiency by clearly communicating expectations and guiding targeted instruction. In mathematics, we use SuccessMaker and MAP assessments to evaluate both content understanding and language use. These tools provide actionable data that help teachers plan interventions, differentiate instruction, and support language acquisition in math contexts. For recently arrived ML/ELLs, ENL providers assess literacy skills in the students’ home language to better understand their academic background and inform instruction. This helps teachers implement appropriate scaffolds and use the students’ native language as a bridge to acquiring English. Students enrolled in Dual Language or Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) programs also take the mCLASS assessment in Spanish, ensuring that their literacy development is monitored in both languages. How do you ensure that MLs/ELLs are appropriately evaluated in their home languages throughout the year? To ensure that Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) are appropriately evaluated in their home languages throughout the year, our school implements targeted assessments aligned to students’ program models and language needs. For Spanish-speaking students in the English as a New Language (ENL) program, ENL teachers administer the Spanish LAB-R to assess language dominance. This data is used to inform instructional planning and ensure that ENL services are tailored to each student's linguistic profile. In the Dual Language (DL) program, students are assessed in both Spanish and English as they develop bilingual proficiency. Formative and summative assessments are used across content areas, and literacy progress in Spanish is monitored through the mCLASS Lecturas screener. This supports the goal of maintaining and strengthening home language skills while developing English proficiency. Similarly, students in the Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program are assessed in Spanish using the mCLASS screener to monitor literacy development in the home language. These assessments ensure that instruction is informed by ongoing data, allowing teachers to make responsive instructional decisions that support language development in both the home language and English.
