What is your NYSESLAT administration plan? How does your school ensure that all ELLs are tested with the NYSESLAT annually? Include the titles of the staff that will administer and score the assessment. Please describe your plan to ensure that all ELLs and former ELLs continue to receive mandated instruction during the testing period. To ensure that all eligible English Language Learners (ELLs) are tested with the NYSESLAT annually, our school has developed a comprehensive administration plan that involves close coordination between school leadership, ENL teachers, the testing coordinator, and instructional coaches. The process begins with generating and reviewing ATS reports (RFSF) to identify all NYSESLAT-eligible students. Additional outreach is conducted to follow up on students with long-term absences to ensure their attendance during the testing window. Once NYSESLAT materials are received, the testing coordinator ensures that all test booklets are accounted for and that answer documents are printed through ATS. All pedagogical staff will receive training from the testing coordinator on how to administer the Listening, Reading, and Writing sections of the NYSESLAT. ENL teachers, along with select additional staff members, will receive specialized training to administer and score the Speaking portion of the assessment. Scoring will be conducted by a trained team of teachers and designated staff members. The testing coordinator and ML/ELL coach will lead the training to ensure that all raters are aligned with NYSESLAT scoring protocols, particularly for Speaking and constructed-response items. To maintain compliance with C.R. Part 154.2 and ensure uninterrupted instructional support during the testing period, our school leverages its staffing model—composed largely of dual-certified ENL/general education and bilingual/general education teachers. These educators will continue to provide mandated services even while supporting testing. ENL teachers will work collaboratively with classroom teachers to embed language supports into daily instruction and share targeted strategies that ensure ELLs and former ELLs continue receiving high-quality instruction aligned to their linguistic and academic needs throughout the NYSESLAT administration period. 18. How does your school ensure that current and former ELLs receive necessary accommodations for state assessments, including the Specialized High School Admissions Test (SHSAT) if applicable? Our school ensures that current and former English Language Learners (ELLs) receive all necessary accommodations for state assessments in ELA, math, and science by following a structured, collaborative, and student-centered approach grounded in New York State guidelines. The process begins with a thorough review of each student’s English language proficiency level, NYSESLAT scores, and ELL status, including former ELLs during the two-years after their score Commanding. Accommodations are determined based on individual student needs and are documented to ensure consistency and compliance. For math and science assessments, eligible ELLs are provided with accommodations such as side-by-side test booklets in English and the student’s home language, when available. This allows students to access content in both languages and better demonstrate their understanding of grade-level material. Bilingual glossaries are also provided during these assessments, giving students access to content-specific terms in both English and their home DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 175 of 250 language. The use of cognates is encouraged as a bridge between languages to support comprehension. In preparation for these assessments, our school integrates these supports into daily instruction throughout the year. Students regularly engage with Tier 2 and Tier 3 academic vocabulary in all subject areas. Instruction includes the use of sentence frames, sentence starters, and paragraph structure outlines to help students develop their academic language and build confidence in reading, writing, and responding to complex tasks. These scaffolds not only support English language development but also ensure that students are familiar with the tools they will use during assessments. In ELA assessments, where fewer accommodations are permitted due to the nature of the test, ELLs still receive supports such as extended time and if needed, a separate testing location to reduce distractions. While bilingual materials are not allowed on the ELA exam, students are supported throughout the year with targeted instruction that builds their ability to analyze texts, write extended responses, and apply academic vocabulary. This preparation ensures they are able to access the assessment with greater independence and confidence. Classroom and ENL teachers collaborate closely throughout the year to ensure that accommodations align with instructional supports. Strategies used during instruction mirror those permitted during testing so that students are comfortable and familiar with the format and tools provided. Prior to the administration of state assessments, the testing coordinator verifies that all accommodations are correctly entered into state systems and provides training to proctors to ensure consistent and appropriate implementation. Throughout the year, teachers work with students on test-prep materials in order to familiarize them with the format of the test. This also allows teachers to monitor the use of accommodations based on students' needs. This practice builds student confidence and ensures that they are able to demonstrate their best understanding of the subject being assessed. Through ongoing differentiation, scaffolded academic language support, and careful planning, our school ensures that all current and former ELLs are equipped with the accommodations they need to demonstrate their knowledge and succeed on state assessments in ELA, math, and science. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 176 of 250 Part VI: Family Partnership Part VI: Family Partnership Family Partnership Required Meetings Under CR Part 154 Describe your schools’ plan to ensure families of ELLs are provided with the required meetings specified below as per CR Part 154. Include: Topics discussed during these meetings How your school ensures families receive necessary translation/interpretation supports to meaningfully engage in the meetings How your school schedules these meetings to facilitate attendance, and how your school maintains evidence of these meetings as required per the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs. a. ELL Program Orientation to inform parents/guardians of newly identified ELLs of ELL program options, including the program goals and requirements for all three ML/ELL program models: Dual Language Bilingual Education, Transitional Bilingual Education and English as a New Language regardless of whether the school currently has either type of bilingual program and provide a high- quality orientation section on the Next Generation Learning Standards, assessments, and school expectations for English Language Learners. Our school has a comprehensive plan to ensure that families of English Language Learners (ELLs) are provided with the required meetings, translation/interpretation supports, and follow-up documentation. As per the Policy and Reference Guide for Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners, newly enrolled students are administered the entire ELL identification process. During registration the Home Language Interview (HLIS) is completed by a trained pedagogue in the preferred language of the parent. After the HLIS is completed the trained pedagogue proceeds with determining if the student has a home language other than English and if the student is eligible to take the NYSITELL. Students who are eligible, are administered the NYSITELL to determine if they need English as a New Language services. Parents and guardians are provided the results of the NYSITELL with an Entitlement letter or a Non-Entitlement letter. As per the results of the NYSITELL, if the student is determined to be an ELL, parents/guardians are invited to the ELL Program Orientation, which is held for families of newly identified ELLs. During this orientation, families are informed of the three state-mandated program models—Dual Language Bilingual Education, Transitional Bilingual Education, and English as a New Language (ENL)—regardless of which programs are currently offered at the school. The orientation also includes an overview of the Next Generation Learning Standards, assessment requirements, and academic expectations for ELLs. Parents are shown the official NYCDOE Parent Orientation video, available in 13 languages, which explains each program model in detail. Supporting informed decision-making is important. Families are provided with the Parent Survey and Program Agreement Form in their preferred language. This ensures that parents understand the program options and are able to select the one that best meets their child’s needs. If the selected program is offered at our school, the student is DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 177 of 250 placed into that program immediately and receives a full schedule aligned with the model. If the chosen program is not available, the school contacts the parent to present the two mandated options: (1) the student may remain at the current school and be placed in an available program, or (2) the student may transfer to a nearby school offering the selected program. The parent’s decision is documented and maintained according to DOE policy. We use multiple outreach strategies to facilitate attendance and family engagement, including flyers, phone calls, and personal invitations. We offer both morning and afternoon sessions, and for families unable to attend in person, we provide virtual or telephone orientation meetings. To ensure families can meaningfully participate, all meetings are supported by translation and interpretation services in the family’s preferred language. Our school also follows DOE guidelines for maintaining evidence of compliance. We keep organized records that include flyers, agendas, sign-in sheets, and copies of the completed Parent Survey and Program Agreement Forms. The original forms are filed in students' cumulative folders, while copies and related documentation are stored in secure program binders for accountability and audit purposes. This system ensures that our school consistently meets all requirements under CR Part 154 and actively supports families in making informed decisions for their children’s education. b. Annual Individual Meeting to discuss goals of the program, language development progress, language proficiency assessment results, and language development needs in all content areas. Note: this meeting is separate from and does not include the mandated ELL program orientation meeting and DOE-scheduled parent-teacher conferences. In compliance with the Policy and Reference Guide for Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners and to foster strong family partnerships, our school ensures that all families of Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs) are provided with an annual individual meeting that is separate from the required ELL Program Orientation and DOE-scheduled parent-teacher conferences. These meetings are designed to provide families with essential information about the goals of the ENL program, their child’s language development progress, language proficiency assessment results (including NYSESLAT), and language development needs across all core content areas. Teachers also use this opportunity to discuss each student’s individual progress in greater detail, offering parents a clear understanding of their child’s academic and linguistic trajectory. To ensure families are able to engage meaningfully, we provide translation and interpretation supports based on the linguistic needs of our school community. Whenever possible, bilingual staff members attend meetings to assist with translation. If a staff member is not available to translate in the parent’s home language, teachers use the NYCDOE Translation and Interpretation Unit’s phone line to provide real-time language assistance, ensuring that all families can fully participate in the conversation. We also have a presentation that is translated into Spanish and Chinese, so that families understand the goals of the ENL program, how their child's language development will progress, the results of their child's English language proficiency assessments, and their child's language development needs in all content areas. These topics are discussed during the required annual individual meeting with all families of ELLs. These meetings are typically scheduled during Parent Engagement Time, allowing for flexible and accessible scheduling. Teachers conduct individual outreach through phone calls, and in-person communication, to coordinate meeting times that best suit each family’s availability. This personalized outreach ensures higher rates of attendance and strengthens the connection between school and home. To document and maintain evidence of compliance, our school uses a standardized parent letter that includes information about the student's current ENL proficiency level. During the meeting, teachers add a personalized comment summarizing the discussion. Parents are then asked to sign the letter, which serves as verification of their attendance and acknowledgment of the meeting’s content. These signed letters are securely stored in accordance with NYCDOE policy, ensuring that our school remains in full compliance with documentation requirements while DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 178 of 250 keeping families informed and engaged in their child’s educational journey. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 179 of 250 Family Engagement 2. Beyond the mandated meetings and orientations, describe how your school: a. Ensures families receive all school communications and documents in the language they best understand. How are families able to respond or initiate communications in languages other than English? Beyond mandated meetings and orientations, our school is committed to fostering inclusive and equitable family engagement by ensuring that all school communications are accessible in the language families best understand. All official correspondence is distributed in English, Spanish, and Chinese, which represent the primary languages spoken within our school community. Communications are sent via email, as well as through platforms such as SMORE, Class Dojo, and Instagram, ensuring that families receive timely updates through multiple channels. To further support two-way communication, families are encouraged to use Class Dojo, which offers automatic language translation features. This allows parents to send messages in their home language, which are then translated into English for the receiving staff member, and vice versa. During any school-based parent meetings, we ensure that bilingual staff members are available to provide live translation when needed. If bilingual staff are not available for a particular language, we utilize the NYCDOE Translation and Interpretation Unit to ensure families can fully participate. These practices ensure that all families feel welcome, informed, and empowered to communicate with the school in a language they understand. b. Develops activities that foster empowerment for families of MLs/ELLs. Include how your school determines the needs of your community. Beyond mandated meetings and orientations, our school is deeply committed to fostering family empowerment by creating inclusive and meaningful engagement opportunities specifically tailored to the needs of our Multilingual Learner (ML) and English Language Learner (ELL) families. All school correspondence is shared in English, Spanish, and Chinese, ensuring accessibility and inclusivity. We use multiple communication platforms—including SMORE, Class Dojo, and Instagram—to keep families informed and involved. In addition, all parent engagement activities are facilitated in these three languages to promote full participation and understanding. To support family empowerment, we offer a wide range of culturally responsive and skill-building activities throughout the year. These include “Welcome Back” Parent Distribution Workshops, Bi-weekly Coffee with the Principal and Parent Coordinator, Weekly Parent Workshops, facilitated by teachers on topics such as Google Classroom, Class Dojo, Envisions, and more, Daily Device Distribution and Maintenance Support, “MyStudent Account” Activation Workshops, Middle School Application Workshops, Lunar New Year Celebration, “Literacy at Home” Parent Workshops, NYSESLAT and ENL Proficiency Level Information Sessions, Raz-Kids and Starfall Digital Literacy Workshops, and Family wellness activities such as Family Yoga. These workshops and events are designed not only to provide academic support but also to build digital literacy, promote cultural inclusion, and strengthen the home-school connection. Our school determines the needs of our community through regular outreach, family surveys, ongoing conversations, and feedback gathered during Coffee with the Principal and other parent forums. This approach allows us to continuously adapt and refine our engagement efforts, ensuring that families of MLs/ELLs feel informed, empowered, and supported throughout the school year. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 180 of 250 c. Ensures families of MLs/ELLs are aware of family leadership opportunities (such as Parent Association (PA), School Leadership Team (SLT), Community Education Council (CEC), etc.) Our school is committed to ensuring that families of Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs) are aware of and encouraged to participate in family leadership opportunities, such as the Parent Association (PA), School Leadership Team (SLT), and Community Education Council (CEC). Invitations to these family leadership opportunities are provided in the language of preference to the families of our MLs/ELLs. In addition, families of ELLs are invited and encouraged to participate in parent volunteer opportunities throughout the school year. Welcoming families to our school allows them an opportunity to participate in a leadership role that promotes social-emotional and academic success. This approach ensures that all families, including those of MLs/ELLs, feel welcomed, informed, and empowered to take on leadership roles within the school community. 3. What culturally and linguistically responsive partnerships do you currently have that support the needs of your school community? Our school actively cultivates a variety of culturally and linguistically responsive partnerships to support and enrich the experiences of our diverse school community. These partnerships are designed to promote cultural awareness, celebrate heritage, and enhance student learning through the arts and community engagement. One of our key partnerships is with the Louis Armstrong House, which provides live jazz performances and interactive opportunities that help students explore different musical genres and cultural histories. We also collaborate with the MIR Organization through their IQ Residency program, which strengthens students' instrumental abilities while educating them about the cultural origins of various musical instruments. To celebrate Chinese heritage, we partner with the New York Chinese Cultural Center, which hosts Lunar New Year performances and offers afterschool classes such as Taekwondo, blending cultural exposure with physical activity. In recognition of African cultures, we host performances by the ZuZu Acrobats, which showcase the traditions of the Bantu culture of East Africa through acrobatics, live music, singing, and dance. In honor of Black History Month, our partnership with Prismatic Magic brings an educational laser light show that highlights the significant contributions of African Americans to U.S. history. For our Newcomer students, we collaborate with the Hispanic Culture Arts organization to create meaningful cultural connections, and through CASA, we partner with Ballet Hispánico to provide instruction in diverse Latin dance styles, helping students deepen their understanding of Hispanic heritage. Additionally, our ongoing relationship with the local 115th Police Precinct enhances our school community through their involvement in events such as Respect for All Week, World Read Aloud Day, the annual Color Run, and the Change Tomorrow Today Wellness Event. These partnerships collectively foster an inclusive, respectful, and culturally rich environment that supports the academic and social-emotional development of all students. Describe the structures and process in place for identification of ELLs year-round as required by the NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, including your school’s identification plan for any incoming students with an Individualized Education Plan (IEP). Include: How your school proactively plans for the ELL identification process so that it does not interrupt the provision of mandated ENL and/or bilingual instruction to current, former and potential ELLs, DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 183 of 250 Who is responsible for each step of the ELL identification process, and School’s plan for ensuring ELLs and former ELLs will continue to receive mandated instruction. Our school maintains a comprehensive, year-round process for identifying and supporting English Language Learners (ELLs), in full alignment with the NYC DOE’s Policy and Reference Guide for Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners. This process ensures timely and accurate identification of all potential ELLs, including students with Individualized Education Programs (IEPs), and guarantees immediate access to appropriate instructional services—whether for current, former, or newly enrolled multilingual learners. To prevent delays in mandated ENL or bilingual instruction, the school has established clear internal procedures. A dedicated ENL/ML Coordinator works closely with the school secretary, testing coordinator, Parent Coordinator and Special Education liaison to monitor new student registrations and identify those who may require ELL services. As soon as a student is flagged as a potential ELL, provisional support begins immediately from ENL teachers, ensuring uninterrupted services while the formal identification process is completed. For students with IEPs, the Language Proficiency Team (LPT) process is promptly initiated to meet the extended 20-day timeline, ensuring accurate evaluation of both language acquisition needs and disability-related services. Following the Policy and Reference Guide, each step in the identification process is assigned to specific staff members to ensure timeliness and accountability. Upon enrollment, the school secretary checks whether a student is new to the New York State public school system or has attended a NYS school within the past two years by reviewing student records in ATS. If prior enrollment is confirmed, the ENL/ML Coordinator contacts the previous school to obtain all relevant data, including NYSITELL or NYSESLAT scores, ELL status, SIFE status, proficiency level, prior ELL service program, parent choice, and state assessment results. This information is then submitted to the ELL Transfers NY department in order to have all data transferred into ATS. If the student has no prior record or has been out of the NYS system for over two years, the full identification process is initiated. A trained pedagogue administers the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) in the parent/guardian’s preferred language. This includes a home language interview with the parent and student. Translation is provided by a bilingual staff member or the DOE Translation and Interpretation Unit if needed. The trained pedagogue determines the student’s home language and NYSTIELL eligibility based on the HLIS parent questionnaire and student interview responses. Once the Home Language Interview is completed, the school’s pupil accounting secretary enters the Home Language code in ATS, and the multilingual coach confirms the entry for accuracy in ATS. If the student's home language is English, the ELL identification process concludes and no further action is needed. If a student with home language other than English is deemed ineligible for NYSITELL testing due to the student interview, the ELL identification process concludes, and ineligibility is recorded in ATS via the ELLO screen. If the student's home language is other than English and is deemed eligible for NYSITELL testing, a trained pedagogue administers the NYSITELL, using test materials printed from the RLBA function in ATS. Results are entered into the system within 10 school days of enrollment—or within 20 school days for students with IEPs. For students with IEPs, the LPT plays a critical role in determining NYSITELL eligibility. The LPT includes the assistant principal, a certified ENL or bilingual teacher, the special education supervisor or designee, the student’s parent or guardian, and an interpreter when needed. The team reviews multiple data points, including the HLIS, the LPT Parent Questionnaire, history of language use at home and in school, special education evaluations, and the IEP itself. If the principal agrees with the LPT recommendation that the student is eligible for the NYSITELL, a trained pedagogue will administer the assessment. If the principal agrees with the LPT recommendation that NYSITELL testing is unnecessary, the decision is sent to the superintendent for final approval. If approved, the ineligibility is recorded in ATS via the ELLO screen. If the superintendent disagrees, the NYSITELL must be administered within five additional school days, and parents must be notified. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 184 of 250 Within five days of determining a student’s ELL status based on NYSITELL results, the ENL/ML Coordinator sends parents the required Entitlement or Non-Entitlement Letter, using DOE-provided templates translated into the family’s preferred language. If the student is newly identified and the home language is Spanish, the Spanish LAB-R is also administered within the same 10-day window to assess Spanish literacy. The school also screens students for potential SIFE (Students with Interrupted or Inconsistent Formal Education) designation. A trained pedagogue completes this process using the NYSED Oral Interview Questionnaire, the Multilingual Literacy SIFE Screener (MLS), and the Writing Screener. SIFE status must be recorded in ATS (BNDC) within 30 school days of enrollment, based on gaps in formal education and performance in literacy and math relative to grade-level expectations. Once a student is identified as an ELL through the results of the NYSITELL, the multilingual coach facilitates a mandatory Parent Orientation. Parents view the DOE’s orientation video, which explains the three ELL program models: English as a New Language (ENL), Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE), and Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE). Afterward, parents complete the Parent Survey and Program Agreement Form to select a program model. If the selected model is available at the school, placement occurs immediately. If not, parents are informed of alternative options, including remaining at the school or transferring to a site that offers their preferred program. All communication is conducted in the parent’s preferred language. The multilingual coach ensures program selection is entered accurately in the ELPC screen in ATS and that placement is finalized within 10 school days of enrollment. A placement confirmation letter is then sent home in the family's preferred language. If, within 45 school days of enrollment, the school has reason to believe a student was misidentified as either an ELL or non-ELL, the Re-Identification Process may be initiated and repeated once. Between six and twelve months after re-identification, the principal reviews the student’s academic progress. If the student has been negatively impacted by the re-identification, the principal, in consultation with qualified staff and the parent, may request a reversal of ELL status, pending superintendent approval. Families receive final notification of any status changes in writing and in their preferred language. To ensure continuity of services, our school maintains an active monitoring system for both current and former ELLs. Current ELLs receive ENL instruction aligned to their proficiency levels, as required under CR Part 154. Former ELLs are monitored for two years following their exit, with regular reviews of both academic and language development. If needed, additional ENL support or academic interventions are provided to ensure continued success. Ongoing collaboration between ENL, general education, and special education staff ensures that all multilingual learners receive equitable, rigorous, and supportive instruction. Through this structured, collaborative, and data-informed approach, our school remains in full compliance with DOE policy and is committed to the academic and linguistic success of every multilingual learner we serve. The ELL Identification Process is completed for each student within 10 school days of enrollment (20 school days for students entering with IEPs). Enrollment status of each newly admitted student is determined: a. If a student has been in New York State public schools within the past 2 years, our school contacts the previous school to obtain ELL status, relevant assessment scores, and English language proficiency level. b. If a student has been outside of New York State public schools for more than 2 years, our school administers the ELL Identification Process as part of reentry. The home language of the student is determined by a trained and licensed pedagogue. a. The parent completes the Home Language Identification Survey in the parent’s preferred language; translation services are provided by a bilingual staff member, the DOE’s over-the-phone translation services, or an alternative, high quality translation service. An interview with parents and students in English and the home language is conducted. This includes reviewing documents, prior assessments, and academic experience. An assessment of the student’s abilities and/or work samples determines the student’s literacy and math level in his/her home language. Eligibility for the NYSITELL is determined. a. If the newly enrolled student is entering with an IEP, the school’s Language Proficiency Team (LPT) recommends eligibility to the principal, who obtains final approval from the superintendent. Students are administered the NYSITELL, if eligible. Notification letters are sent to the parent/guardian in their preferred language. a. Parent is notified of their child’s ELL status and results of the NYSITELL. If the student is identified as an ELL and the student’s home language is Spanish, the student is administered the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (LAB). Student with Interrupted/Inconsistent Formal Education (SIFE) status is determined using New York State’s resources. If a student is a newly identified ELL, the parent is invited to the parent orientation meeting. a. The parent orientation meeting provides parents/guardians with an explanation of each ELL program model offered in NYC; the parent video describing the three programs is presented (in parent’s preferred language); parents/guardians are encouraged to ask questions so that they are DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 186 of 250 informed about all ELL programs; translation and interpretation services are provided throughout the meeting. ELL is placed in the ELL program that the parent selected. a. If the bilingual program that the parent selected is not available, the parent is offered a transfer to a school with that bilingual program in accordance with NYC DOE transfer policies. b. If the ELL remains in the school, the ELL is placed in English as a New Language and our school keeps track of parent selection so that when minimum thresholds are met, we can open the ELL program of choice. Parents are notified that they have 45 days to appeal the ELL status of their child (ELL Re-identification Process). If the ELL Re-identification Process is implemented for any student, the principal reviews the decision of any student whose ELL status was changed because of the ELL Re-identification Process within 6 to 12 months. Copies of documentation regarding ELL identification, including letters and parent notifications are kept in the students’ cumulative files. In the event of a school-wide emergency—such as a lockdown, fire, student attendance concern, or medical situation—our school is committed to ensuring timely and accurate communication with all parents in their preferred language. Our primary communication strategy includes direct outreach via telephone, utilizing our bilingual out-of-classroom teachers, paraprofessionals, and other multilingual staff members to provide interpretation in Spanish and other commonly spoken languages. This multi-tiered approach ensures that families are informed and supported, regardless of the language they speak. We are committed to maintaining open and equitable lines of communication with all families, especially during critical situations. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 195 of 250 Part D: Staff Awareness Part D: Staff Awareness Staff Awareness Plan to ensure staff awareness of language access requirements and available resources. 1. Describe how your school will ensure that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School-based Staff on the WeLearnNYC learning platform, and are familiar with the Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents and Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents. Additionally, explain how your Galaxy-designated LAC will disseminate future language access resources and updates to all staff members. To ensure that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School- Based Staff on the WeLearnNYC learning platform, our school will communicate this requirement clearly at the start of the school year. Staff will be provided designated time during professional development days to complete the training. Completion will be tracked using an internal Excel spreadsheet, maintained by the school’s administration team, to ensure full compliance. In addition to the training, all staff will receive guidance on the Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents and Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents. This information will be covered during staff orientation and training sessions, which will be conducted either in-person or remotely at the beginning of the school year. To support ongoing awareness and implementation of language access protocols, staff will receive digital copies of language access resources through email and a shared drive accessible to all team members. Our Galaxy- designated Language Access Coordinator (LAC) will be responsible for disseminating any future language access resources, updates, or changes in NYCPS policy. These updates will be shared promptly via staff-wide emails and uploaded to the shared drive to ensure staff have continual access to the most current information and guidance. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 196 of 250 Part E: Parental Notification Part E: Parental Notification Parental Notification Provision of notice to parents on the available language assistance services. 1. Describe how your school will inform parents about available language assistance services in their preferred language. Include any of the following: Translated signage Brochures, flyers, or letters shared with parents Parent engagement events where language assistance services are highlighted (e.g., PTA meetings, international parents’ night, etc.) Messaging applications Language Access for NYC Public Schools Families brochure Our school is committed to ensuring that all families are informed about the language assistance services available to them in their preferred language. To achieve this, we will utilize multiple strategies and communication channels throughout the school year. The NYCPS Translation and Interpretation Policy will be introduced to families during key parent engagement events, such as Parent Teacher Conferences, mandated ELL parent meetings, Parent Selection Orientations, and school- wide events like International Families Night and PTA meetings. This information will also be regularly shared through our school’s social media platforms, including Instagram, and via the ClassDojo messaging application. At the beginning of the school year, families will receive printed materials outlining available language assistance services in their preferred language. Prior to any in-person or remote parent meetings, families will be reminded that interpretation services are available through the DOE for languages beyond Spanish and Chinese, which are commonly used during our meetings. To ensure clear visibility and accessibility, the “Welcome Poster,” “I Speak” cards, and Language Identification (ID) Cards will be displayed prominently at the main entrance near the security desk. Additional copies of the “I Speak” cards and the Language ID Guide will be available in the main office, along with multilingual parent brochures. Furthermore, resources such as The Parent’s Guide to Language Access and the NYC Public School Guidebook will be shared digitally through ClassDojo to ensure all families have ongoing access to this critical information. Through these combined efforts, we aim to foster an inclusive and informed school community where every family feels welcomed and supported, regardless of the language they speak. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 197 of 250 Part F: Monitoring Part F: Monitoring Monitoring Methods for soliciting and implementing feedback on language assistance services. 1. In addition to the feedback received from parents via OLA’s Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey, describe the methods your school will use to gather feedback from parents regarding the language assistance services they receive in their preferred language. Gathering feedback from parents is essential to improving the quality and effectiveness of our language assistance services. In addition to reviewing responses from the Office of Language Access (OLA)’s Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey, our school uses a variety of methods to collect input directly from families in their preferred languages. Each year, we analyze data from the Parent’s Preferred Language Report (RCPL) to identify the home languages of our families. This information is instrumental in shaping our communication strategies and ensuring that all families have equitable access to school documents, services, and events. To facilitate meaningful feedback, staff members are available to assist parents in completing surveys or forms in their native language. When a bilingual staff member is not available for a specific language, we utilize the NYCPS Over-the-Phone Interpretation (OPI) service to support families in real time. This ensures that all parents are able to share their experiences and voice concerns regarding language access without barriers. In addition, feedback is regularly collected during parent engagement events such as Parent-Teacher Conferences, ELL parent meetings, and “Coffee with the Principal.” During these gatherings, parents are encouraged to share their experiences with school communication, translation, and interpretation services. This feedback is reviewed by the School Administration and relevant stakeholders to inform ongoing improvements. Through these consistent and inclusive practices, we are committed to ensuring that all families feel heard, respected, and supported in their preferred language. 2. How has your school worked to improve its language assistance services? Share any feedback results and outline your plans for further improvements in the upcoming school year. To foster an inclusive and supportive environment for all families, our school has taken intentional steps to improve language assistance services. The School Administration, in collaboration with the Parent Coordinator, Community Associate, STH Community Coordinator, and ML/ELL Coach, has established ongoing channels of communication to gather parent feedback and identify effective practices to enhance language access. Monthly meetings hosted by the Administration and Parent Coordinator in partnership with the PTA provide a structured space to discuss parent needs and concerns. Feedback collected through these meetings is reviewed DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 198 of 250 collaboratively by School Administration, the Parent Coordinator, and the ML/ELL Coach to inform the development of school-wide initiatives that address language access and family engagement. One such initiative is “Coffee with the Principal,” a bi-monthly open forum led by the Principal and other key staff members. This event is designed to amplify parent voice, foster dialogue, and provide parents with valuable resources to support their children at home. Presentations from the Guidance Department, ML/ELL Coach, and AIS team are regularly featured to address topics of interest and ensure families are equipped with helpful tools and information. To further strengthen language support, the Administration actively identifies bilingual staff members and offers flexible scheduling to accommodate their roles in oral interpretation and written translation. In cases where a specific home language is not represented by school staff, we utilize the Office of Language Access and Over-the- Phone Interpretation (OPI) services to ensure families receive timely and accurate communication in their preferred language. All school-wide communication is translated or interpreted accordingly. These initiatives have been well received by families, as evidenced by increased participation in school events and positive feedback regarding improved accessibility. Moving forward, we will continue to implement and refine these practices, using ongoing parent feedback to guide our efforts and expand our capacity to meet the diverse language needs of our school community. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 199 of 250 Title III Outline Title III Outline Title III Supplemental Program for ELLs for SY 2025-26 REQUIREMENTS Title III supplemental services for English Language Learners (ELLs) must include the following three components: Direct instruction activities must be used to support language development, English and home language instruction, high academic achievement in math, and/or high academic achievement in other core academic areas. The Title III supplemental instructional services must be based on student need. These supplemental services should complement core bilingual and ENL services required under CR Part 154. Direct supplemental services should be provided for before school, after school, and Saturday programs. Teachers providing the services must be bilingual education and/or English for Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) certified teachers. High quality professional development that is "of sufficient intensity and duration to have a positive and lasting impact on the teachers’ performance in classrooms." Professional development activities should be well-planned, ongoing events rather than one-day or short-term workshops and conferences. Parent engagement and supports must ensure that there are appropriate translation and interpretation services to meet community needs. These are in addition to mandated activities, such as parent orientation during ELL identification process or annual ELL parent meetings. Note: The Title III ELL program planning ratio is as follows: 60% to direct instruction, 10% to professional development (if funded using Title III ELL funds), 10% to parent engagement (if funded using Title III ELL funds), and 20% to OTPS (if funded using Title III ELL funds). For more information on Title III requirements, please see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. Any updates or revisions to this plan must be made through the iPlan portal. Our Title III-funded programs for the 2025–2026 school year will center on the Newcomer December/February/April PBL Enrichment Camp, Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program and Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Media, Chess, Arts). These Title III-funded programs are designed to provide additional academic and language development support to English Language Learners (ELLs). Our programs specifically target Newcomer Multilingual Learners/ELLs (0–3 years) and Developing ELLs (4-6 years of ENL services). According to the October 2025 RLER data, our school is composed of a total of 661 Newcomers and 180 Developing ELLs. Students in the program will be grouped heterogeneously by grade and proficiency level. Classes will be led by an ENL-certified teacher. Instruction will be delivered in English, with strategic use of students' home languages for support. The Newcomer December/February/April PBL Enrichment Camp, will run during the December, February, and April school recesses from 8:00 AM to 1:00 PM, and will serve 180 ELLs in grades K–5. The Newcomer December/February/April PBL Enrichment Camp is grounded in Project-Based Learning (PBL) to support literacy development, academic content knowledge, and social-emotional learning. Through integrated PBL units, students will explore themes across content areas, including science and social studies, while actively engaging in listening, speaking, reading, and writing tasks. Students will be grouped heterogeneously utilizing ELL data and grade level. There will be six groups of 32 students in each. Instruction will follow an integrated ENL self-contained teaching model or a general education and ENL service provider co-teaching model. The Language Enrichment Clubs and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts programs will run either before school, after school or Saturdays. These after school programs will serve 140 students, 25 students in Cheer, 25 in Media, 25 in Chess, 25 in Art, and 40 students in the theater club in grades K–5. The Language Enrichment Clubs and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program will support students in their content-area learning, language development and their understanding of grade-appropriate skills, in addition to supporting social/emotional learning. The curriculum for our programs will also incorporate core instructional tools such as Heggerty Phonemic Awareness, Orton-Gillingham, and HMH Into Reading/Arriba la Lectura, which are used during the regular school day to maintain instructional continuity. Students will be grouped heterogeneously utilizing ELL data and grade level with 25 students in Cheer, 25 in Media, 25 in Chess, 25 in Art, and 40 students in the theater club in grades K–5. Instruction will follow an ENL self-contained teaching model or a general education and ENL service provider co- teaching model. Throughout each session for each of the Title III Programs, students will engage in hands-on, collaborative activities that support their academic and language growth. Activities in these programs will promote cognitive development aligned to the Next Generation Learning Standards and foster the use of metacognitive strategies, including self- assessment, monitoring tools, and student rubrics. Instructional strategies taught in these programs will include activating background knowledge, contextualizing new information through realia, utilizing graphic supports and verbal analogies, and building schema through meaningful connections to students’ lived experiences. All activities will aim to accelerate students' progress through the language acquisition continuum and be tailored to students’ language proficiency and academic needs. Student progress in each of our Title III Programs will be monitored using a combination of formal and informal assessments. Engagement and learning outcomes during the enrichment sessions will also be assessed through teacher observations and student project completion. At the end DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 203 of 250 of each of these Title III Programs, families will be invited to a presentation, where the projects the students worked on in each of these programs will be showcased. Each of the Title III Programs will be overseen by a designated site supervisor, who will manage daily operations, including maintaining student attendance, compiling records, and communicating with families. Invitations and progress updates will be provided to families in their preferred language and will be documented accordingly. Supervision of the Title III programs is at no cost to Title III ELL funds. By offering targeted instruction in a supportive and engaging environment, these Title III-funded enrichment programs will continue to promote equitable access to rigorous instruction for our ML/ELL students, fostering both academic success and long-term language proficiency. Rationale for Title III ELL Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III ELL funded program. For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program The rationale for PS 143’s Title III-funded programs is rooted in both qualitative observations and quantitative data that underscore the continued need to support English language acquisition and foundational literacy skills among our Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners (MLs/ELLs), with a particular focus on our Newcomer and Developing ELL population. According to the October 2025 RLER data our school serves 661 Newcomers and 185 Developing ELLs. Our programs specifically target Newcomer Multilingual Learners/ELLs (0–3 years) and Developing ELLs (4-6 years of ENL services), and are performing at the Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, or Expanding levels. Based on October 2025 RLAT results, our student population includes 154 students at the Entering level, 214 at the Emerging level, 247 at the Transitioning level, and 233 at the Expanding level. We analyzed these data sources and noticed that the ELLs in our school need further support in the modalities of Speaking and Writing. The Title III Programs in our school focus on these modalities, so that students continue to improve their English language acquisition. These students are enrolled across ENL, Dual Language, and Transitional Bilingual Education programs, and include both English Proficient students and ELLs with special needs. Many of our students continue to experience the residual effects of interrupted learning due to the COVID-19 pandemic and school closures. As a result, targeted support is necessary to accelerate their language acquisition and strengthen foundational literacy skills. Project-Based Learning (PBL), language enrichment clubs and language enriched theater programs have proven to be effective at PS 143, particularly in enhancing students’ English language development and overall engagement. Teachers have observed a notable increase in student participation and confidence in speaking, listening, reading and writing when engaged in PBL units and other enrichment activities. This approach provides students with authentic opportunities to use language meaningfully across content areas, promoting both academic and social-emotional growth. These outcomes affirm the need for a continued and expanded enrichment programs outside of the school day, to address the diverse linguistic needs of our ML/ELL population and ensure their progress toward English proficiency. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 209 of 250 Part C: Professional Development Part C: Professional Development Professional Development Summary Provide a summary of your school’s plan for professional development under your Title III ELL program. The school’s professional development plan under the Title III ELL program is designed to strengthen the instructional practices of educators working with Multilingual and English Language Learners (ML/ELLs). The initiative focuses on equipping teachers with effective strategies and resources to better support the language development and academic success of their students. As part of the December/February/April Project-Based Learning Enrichment Camps, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts) and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program, participating teachers will engage in ongoing training throughout the school year that emphasizes English as a New Language (ENL) skills, curriculum implementation, and ENL pedagogy. Teachers will receive targeted instruction in effective literacy strategies tailored to the needs of ELLs, ensuring that students build strong foundations in the modalities of listening, speaking, reading and writing. Training will also include sessions focused on utilizing project-based learning to support content, enhance classroom engagement, and continue language development. All professional development activities, including training sessions and related materials, will be at no cost to the Title III ELL funds. Through this comprehensive professional development plan, the school aims to build educator capacity and create a more inclusive, supportive, and effective learning environment for ML/ELL students. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 210 of 250 Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. The rationale for the professional development provided under our Title III ELL program is rooted in both qualitative and quantitative data that highlight the need for targeted support in foundational literacy, ENL instructional strategies, and compliance with state mandates regarding ML/ELLs. Based on October 2025 RLAT results, our student population includes 154 students at the Entering level, 214 at the Emerging level, 247 at the Transitioning level, and 233 at the Expanding level. Additionally, October 2025 RLER data indicates that our school serves approximately 661 Newcomers and 185 Developing ELLs. To effectively serve our diverse student population, all teachers that work the Title III program, must be equipped with a strong understanding of ENL strategies, state regulations, and the ELL identification process. Professional development is therefore an ongoing, school-wide initiative delivered throughout the year by certified ENL teachers, dual language teachers, instructional supervisors, and our Bilingual/ENL Compliance point person, along with a DOE-contracted Orton-Gillingham (OG) consultant who provides structured literacy training. These sessions are strategically scheduled during designated common planning times and professional development periods, with monthly agendas and sign-in documentation to ensure accountability. When analyzing our data sources, such as the RLAT for our ELLs overall proficiency levels and proficiency in each modality, in addition to mClass data, we noticed that our ELLs need additional support in the modalities of Speaking and Writing. As a result, we host professional Development sessions, in which teachers analyze student ELL data to identify specific trends. Other sessions focus on the use of resources, scaffolds and strategies to promote speaking and writing in multiple, complete sentences. In addition, professional development is centered around student-led discussions, emphasizing vocabulary development and utilizing various structures. Staff learn how to implement a student-friendly NYSESLAT-aligned speaking rubric, which is a tool students can use to develop their proficiency in the speaking modality. To support students' development of writing skills, professional development sessions also focus on utilizing various graphic organizers, sentence structures, and creating a cohesive response when writing. The impact of professional development is measured qualitatively through PD self-reflections, ongoing in-house teacher surveys, and action plans, and quantitatively through analysis of student performance data, including mCLASS (K–5), MAP (K–5), and state exam results. Trends in our data—such as persistent challenges in foundational literacy skills observed through mCLASS and NYSESLAT results—indicate a clear need for continued professional learning in this area. Moreover, as our school works toward implementing school-wide protocols aligned with Hallmark #2 of the Instructional Focus—promoting talk and discussion to build both conversational and academic language—our Title III students have demonstrated ongoing reading growth, further supporting the effectiveness of our PD sessions. In addition to foundational literacy and ENL-focused training, professional development includes sessions on project-based learning, where teachers explore the importance of hands-on, student-centered instruction that supports both academic content and language acquisition. These sessions guide educators in designing and implementing PBL experiences that encourage collaboration, critical thinking, and authentic use of language—key components in helping ML/ELLs develop both conversational and academic proficiency. Teachers to Receive Professional Development Include information on the teachers to receive professional development. Please specify how all teachers working in the Title III ELL program will receive ongoing professional development related to DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 211 of 250 the Title III ELL program. All teachers involved in the Title III ELL December/February/April Project-Based Learning Enrichment Camps, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts), and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program will receive ongoing professional development focused on effective Project-Based Learning (PBL) strategies to better support ML/ELL students. This includes certified ENL teachers, general education teachers with ML/ELLs in their classrooms, dual language educators, and support staff. Professional development will focus on the design and implementation of project-based units that promote inquiry, collaboration, and meaningful language use. Teachers will explore how PBL fosters deeper engagement and accelerates language development by situating learning in real-world, culturally relevant contexts. Training sessions will include modeling of PBL practices, planning time, and collaborative reflection to ensure alignment with ENL instructional goals. By equipping all Title III ELL program educators with the tools to integrate PBL into their practice, we aim to create language-rich, student-centered learning environments that enhance both academic outcomes and language proficiency for our multilingual learners. As part of our ongoing commitment to supporting Multilingual and English Language Learners (ML/ELLs), our school’s professional development plan under the Title III ELL program includes a structured calendar of targeted learning opportunities for educators throughout the year. The learning opportunities include Orton-Gillingham training, mClass data analysis, and utilizing ENL data from the NYSESLAT. These sessions are designed to build instructional capacity in foundational literacy, ENL pedagogy, curriculum implementation, and the use of data to inform instruction, with a particular emphasis on the integration of Project-Based Learning (PBL) strategies. These sessions are strategically scheduled during designated common planning times and professional development periods, with monthly agendas and sign-in documentation to ensure accountability. Throughout the year, all Title III ELL program educators—including ENL and general education teachers, dual language educators, and support staff—will participate in these ongoing professional learning opportunities. By combining literacy-focused training, PBL integration, and data-driven reflection, this plan ensures that teachers are equipped to meet the diverse needs of ML/ELLs and foster both language development and academic achievement. Topics to be covered by month, Participants participating in Title III programs receiving training, DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 213 of 250 Month of September Topic 1- mCLASS Universal Screener - In this session teachers revisited the core purpose of administering the Universal Screener: mCLASS along with the importance of maintaining fidelity during assesments. Each grade band will explore the various measures being administered and examples of how students are expected to respond. Participants will receive testing window information and coding of student answers. Month of October Topic 2- Analyzing NYSESLAT Data and Trends- In this session, coaches will facilitate a data-driven analysis focused on supporting ENL students. The session will begin with sharing a school-wide ENL data snapshot, followed by individualized data from the NYSITELL and NYSESLAT. Teachers will have the opportunity to analyze their own class data, identifying patterns, trends, and individual student needs. A key component of the session is using the ENL Data Planner to help teachers group students based on the proficiency level for each modality (listening, speaking, reading, writing). With these groupings, teachers will be able to plan differentiated scaffolds and resources, tailored to the specific modalities students need to demonstrate their understanding of lesson content. This ensures that instruction would be responsive to the varying needs of students, allowing for targeted support based on how they engage with the lesson and demonstrate mastery of the skills being taught. Topic 3 - Training for the programs such as OG (Part 1)- Participants will receive an introduction to Orton- Gillingham–aligned practices, exploring foundational principles and instructional routines that support structured literacy for multilingual learners. Topic 4- Analyzing BOY assessments with mCLASS- Teachers will engage in a data-dive using BOY mCLASS benchmark results. Each grade band will analyze raw scores from the specified probes to determine the levels in which their students attained using the benchmark ranges. Thereafter, teachers will use Cycle 1 criteria to select students in need of Tier 2 intervention, along with plotting the precise location of how students performed within the bucket of ranges from Above to Well Below benchmark. Month of November Topic 3 - Training for the programs such as OG (Part 2)- Participants will receive an introduction to Orton- Gillingham–aligned practices, exploring foundational principles and instructional routines that support structured literacy for multilingual learners. Topic 5 - Analyzing FURBY Report - The session will include an overview of FURBY data reports, where educators will be introduced to this data tool and how it can be used to analyze student progress. FURBY data reports provide a detailed snapshot of student performance, which helps educators identify trends, track growth, and make data- informed instructional decisions. Following the overview, the group will engage in a Data Analysis Protocol. Teachers will be guided through a structured process to interpret and analyze the data. The protocol emphasizes identifying and interpreting patterns in student performance, as well as implications for instruction. One key focus of the data analysis is the identification of "Cusp" and "Idle Performers" Students. These students are typically those who are on the verge of a significant shift in their learning trajectory- moving up to the next proficiency level and students who remained at the same proficiency level. The group will discuss strategies to support these cusp students, ensuring that they receive the appropriate scaffolds and opportunities to break through to the next level of proficiency. December Topic 6- Learning more about PBL (Rationale, Project Selection and Planning)- This session will provide an overview of Project-Based Learning, helping participants understand its impact on language development and begin planning meaningful, standards-aligned projects. Month of January Topic 7- Learning more about PBL (Rationale, Project Selection and Planning) - This session will provide an overview DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 214 of 250 of Project-Based Learning, helping participants understand its impact on language development and begin planning meaningful, standards-aligned projects. Topic 8- Language Objectives- In this session teachers will deepen their understanding of effective language objectives by exploring each of their key components—language function, domain, supports, and content connection. The session will support participants in crafting clear, measurable language objectives aligned to content lessons, ensuring intentional support for listening, speaking, reading, and writing. Teachers will engage in crafting clear, measurable language objectives aligned to content lessons, ensuring intentional support for the target modality listening, speaking, reading, and writing. Month of February Topic 9- Analyzing Growth using mCLASS data - Participants will compare beginning- and mid-year mCLASS results to identify growth patterns, adjust instructional strategies, and plan targeted supports for students. Topic 10 - Review of NYSESLAT Listening and Speaking Sections (Focus on the Speaking rubric)- Teachers in grades K - 5 will gain a better understanding of the NYSESLAT Speaking Rubric by analyzing indicators for each proficiency level. This session deepens understanding of the listening and speaking components of the NYSESLAT, with special attention to evaluating student responses using the speaking rubric. Month of March Topic 11- Learning more about PBL (Rationale, Project Selection and Planning)- This session will provide an overview of Project-Based Learning, helping participants understand its impact on language development and begin planning meaningful, standards-aligned projects. Month of April Topic 12- Analyzing Trends using mCLASS data - In this session, teachers will analyze mCLASS PM data to assess student and group progress, identifying strengths and areas of need for targeted Tier 2 instruction. Based on this data, teachers will select an intensive group for Cycle 3, focusing on students who require additional support in specific areas such as phonics, fluency, or comprehension. They will then create a SMART goal for this group that outlines clear, measurable outcomes students should achieve by the end of the next 6-week cycle, ensuring alignment with instructional objectives and guiding the focus of Tier 2 interventions moving forward. " Topic 13- Review of NYSESLAT Reading and Writing Sections (Focus on the Writing rubric)- Teachers in grades K - 5 will gain a better understanding of the NYSESLAT Writing Rubric by analyzing indicators for each proficiency level and dimension. This session deepens understanding of the reading and writing components of the NYSESLAT, with special attention to evaluating student responses using the writing rubric. Month of June- Topic 14- Analyzing EOY data using mCLASS- This final session focuses on reviewing end-of-year mCLASS data to measure overall student progress, reflect on program effectiveness, and plan instructional priorities for the upcoming school year. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 217 of 250 Part D: Family Engagement Activities Part D: Family Engagement Activities Family Engagement Summary Provide a summary of your school’s family engagement activities under your Title III ELL program. Describe the activities that families of ELLs will engage with by topic. Please make sure to include whether the activities will be funded by Title III ELL funds. Please note: If family engagement activities will be funded by Title III ELL funds, vendor cost is calculated as part of OTPS and must not exceed 20% of the total allocation; teacher per-session is calculated as part of family engagement and must not exceed 10% of the total allocation. Family engagement is a central component of our Title III ELL programs, rooted in the belief that strong partnerships between schools and families are essential for the academic, social, and emotional success of Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs). Our parent liaison and school counselors provide a robust series of monthly in-person workshops, designed to assist parents with ensuring their child's social, emotional, and academic success. Additionally, Coffee with the Principal/PTA is also offered monthly throughout the year to discuss various important topics related to the school and students. Workshops are led by a collaborative team that includes ENL teachers, site supervisors, and instructional coaches. Topics covered include understanding language acquisition stages, language development strategies, navigating school resources and supports, strategies to promote literacy at home, and social-emotional development. Prior to the launch of any Title III program, families are invited to an orientation where they are informed of program goals, expectations, and the supports available to help their children succeed. Parents of students enrolled in the December/February/April Project-Based Learning Enrichment Camps, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts), and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program, will receive invitations at the end of each program to participate in the showcasing of projects presented by students. To celebrate our English Language Learners, honor their diverse cultural backgrounds, and support their unique needs, our school hosts a variety of parent engagement events. These include our Travel the World Cultural Fair, Painting for Students Celebration, and initiatives through our Newcomer Center. These events will be at cost to the Title III ELL Funds. All parent engagement sessions are conducted in a welcoming, inclusive environment. Spanish-speaking staff are always on-site to provide interpretation, and Chinese interpretation is available upon request. For families who speak other languages, Over-the-Phone Interpretation (OPI) services are provided to ensure that all parents receive information in their preferred language. All communication, including invitations, reminders, and follow-up materials, are distributed in families’ home languages and maintained on file by our Parent Liaison, along with attendance sheets for each session. These workshops are at cost to Title III ELL funds. Through these efforts, we aim to foster a culturally responsive and inclusive school community in which families feel empowered, informed, and actively involved in their children's language development and academic success. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 218 of 250 Rationale for Family Engagement Activities Describe the rationale for the family engagement activities to be provided: For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., reasons for topics, time activities will be offered), and Data sources (e.g., parent survey responses, in-house parent surveys, PTA/PA request, etc.) used to justify the need for activities. To ensure that family engagement activities are purposeful and responsive to the needs of our multilingual learner (ML/ELL) families, our school relies on both quantitative and qualitative data sources. According to the September 2025 RFSF report, our student population includes 163 students at the Entering level, 208 at Emerging, 246 at Transitioning, and 234 at Expanding, based on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT and Fall 2025 NYSITELL assessments. These language proficiency levels highlight the importance of offering targeted support to families across a broad spectrum of English development. Parent interest and needs are identified through in-house surveys, teacher and staff feedback, and input from the PTA/PA. Survey responses collected at the beginning of the school year helped us determine topics of interest and preferences for engagement formats and times. One key area identified by families and staff was the need for a better understanding of the NYSESLAT exam. As a result, workshops were designed to introduce parents to the structure, purpose, and preparation strategies for the NYSESLAT across grade levels. These workshops provide opportunities for families to engage with sample materials and ask questions that support their child’s academic growth. To support language development through enrichment, we offer overviews for families of students participating in Project-Based Learning (PBL) Enrichment Camps, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts) and in our Language Enriched Theater and Arts program. These sessions explain how each program integrates English language development in fun, engaging ways aligned with student interests. Recognizing the unique needs of our recently arrived students and their families. In connection with our programs, families are invited to visit the school to view their children’s work, observe their academic progress, and gain a deeper appreciation of their development in English language skills. This experience strengthens home-school connections and encourages continued language learning at home. Parents of students enrolled in the programs will receive invitations at the end of each program to participate in showcases featuring student projects. To support our newly arrived students and their families, The Newcomers Center workshop is designed to address the unique needs of recently arrived students and their families, who often require additional support navigating the school system and accessing resources. Data from the NYSITELL assessments and registration interviews indicate a significant number of newcomers who benefit from targeted support. Feedback from teachers and parents has emphasized the importance of providing families with information about available resources, including language assistance and community connections. By equipping families with strategies to support their children’s language growth and engagement, the Newcomers Center fosters a welcoming and inclusive environment that promotes equity and belonging for all students. In addition to academic and informational workshops, we host our Travel the World Cultural Fair, a school-wide celebration that honors the diverse cultures within our community. Teachers host interactive booths that allow families to explore global traditions, art, music, food, and crafts while building cross-cultural understanding and community connection. This event also serves as a joyful way for families to see themselves and their cultures reflected in the school environment. To ensure equitable access and participation, all communications are provided in English, Spanish, and Chinese, with interpretation available in additional languages as needed. Family input is continually gathered through PTA/PA DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 219 of 250 meetings, “Coffee with the Principal” sessions, and one-on-one meetings with teachers. These platforms allow parents to share their ideas, questions, and concerns, which directly influence the planning and refinement of future events. The School Leadership Team includes parent representatives who collaborate with school staff to ensure family perspectives are consistently considered in school decision-making. Finally, we recognize the importance of building ongoing relationships between families and school staff. ENL teachers regularly meet with families during office hours and school events to discuss student progress, language goals, and individual successes. These experiences foster stronger home-school connections and encourage continued language learning beyond the classroom. Together, these thoughtfully planned activities reflect a comprehensive, data-informed approach to family engagement that values the voices, experiences, and contributions of all families in our school community.
