DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 224 of 250 Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Application for Immigrant Funding REQUIREMENTS Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds can be used for activities that provide enhanced instructional opportunities for immigrant students, which include: Identification and acquisition of curricular materials, educational software and technologies to be used in the program. Family literacy, parent outreach and training activities designed to assist parents in becoming active participants in the education of their children and understand their rights and the benefits of multilingualism. Support for personnel, including teacher aides, parent coordinators, and school counselors who have been specifically trained, or are being trained, to provide services to immigrant students and families. Provision of tutorials, mentoring, and academic or career counseling for immigrant students, as well as engagement in pre-college and work-based learning experiences. Basic instructional services directly attributable to the aforementioned school districts, including costs for additional classroom supplies, transportation costs, or other costs directly attributable to such services. Other instructional services that are designed to assist immigrant students to achieve in elementary schools and secondary schools in the United States, such as programs of introduction to the educational system and civics education. School activities, coordinated with community-based organizations, institutions of higher education, private sector entities, or other entities with expertise in working with immigrants, to assist families of immigrant students by offering comprehensive community services. Funding follows similar guidelines for Title III funds established by the Office of Multilingual Learners (OML) and can only be used to provide supplementary services to immigrant students, who may or may not be English Language Learners (ELLs). Please note: Immigrant students are not necessarily English Language Learners (ELLs), and ELLs may or may not be immigrants. For the purpose of this funding, immigrant students are defined as individuals who were not born in any U.S. state (this includes the District of Columbia and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico); and have not been attending one or more schools in any one or more states for more than three full, academic years (the months need not be consecutive). DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 225 of 250 Priority areas for the use of Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds are as follows: Extended day programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to provide targeted, small-group instruction, credit recovery support, and postsecondary preparation. Implementing strong student supports to increase graduation rates. Community-based and school-based programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to strengthen partnerships between immigrant families and schools. Developing new and/or enhancing existing programs for newcomers (meeting criteria defined above). Supplementing the opening or growth of Transitional Bilingual Education programs and/or Dual Language Bilingual Education programs serving immigrant students. Professional learning on meeting the needs of immigrant students, creating a welcoming and inclusive school environment, and effective instructional practices. For more information on Title III requirements, refer to NYSED’s guidance on allowable and unallowable expenditures for Title III. For specific allocation amounts per school, see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. DIRECTIONS Please complete the application below. Schools must use the funds to support their immigrant students by developing a plan that has each of the following components: Direct instruction, supplemental program for immigrant students, Professional development that bolsters the instructional practice of teachers of immigrant students, and Family engagement activities that support families of immigrant students. Completed applications must be submitted in iPlan for review and approval. Please refer to the respective School Allocation Memo. Schools are not permitted to use funds until they submit a plan and receive notification of its approval. Our Title III Immigrant-funded programs for the 2025–2026 school year will center on the Newcomer December/February/April PBL Enrichment Camp, Saturday/Sunday Academy, Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program and Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts). These Title III Immigrant - funded programs are designed to provide additional academic and language development support to English Language Learners (ELLs). Title III Immigrant students in our programs are those who were not born in the United States and who have not attended school in the U.S. for more than three full academic years. Based on the 2025/2026 School Year 2026 RFSF and RPOB results, our student population includes 409 immigrant ELLs, with 117 (28.6%) students at the Entering level, 118 (28.9%) at the Emerging level, 105 (25.7%) at the Transitioning level, and 69 (16.8%) at the Expanding level. Students in the program will be grouped heterogeneously by grade and proficiency level. Classes will be led by an ENL-certified teacher. Instruction will be delivered in English, with strategic use of students' home DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 228 of 250 languages for support. The Newcomer December/February/April PBL Enrichment Camp, will run during the December, February, and April school recesses from 8:00 AM to 1:00 PM, and will serve 180 immigrant ELLs in grades K–5. The Newcomer December/February/April PBL Enrichment Camp is grounded in Project-Based Learning (PBL) to support literacy development, academic content knowledge, and social-emotional learning. Through integrated PBL units, students will explore themes across content areas, including science and social studies, while actively engaging in listening, speaking, reading, and writing tasks. Students will be grouped heterogeneously utilizing ELL data and grade level. There will be six groups of 30 immigrant students in each. Instruction will follow an integrated ENL self-contained teaching model or a general education and ENL service provider co-teaching model. The Language Enrichment Clubs and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts programs will run after school and Saturdays. These after school programs will serve 100 students in grades K–5. The Language Enrichment Clubs and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program will support students in their content-area learning, language development and their understanding of grade-appropriate skills, in addition to supporting social/emotional learning. The curriculum for our programs will also incorporate core instructional tools such as Heggerty Phonemic Awareness, Orton-Gillingham, and HMH Into Reading/Arriba la Lectura, which are used during the regular school day to maintain instructional continuity. Students will be grouped heterogeneously utilizing ELL data and grade level with 25 immigrant students in each group. Instruction will follow an ENL self-contained teaching model or a general education and ENL service provider co-teaching model. Students in the Saturday/Sunday academy will engage in a data driven instruction around literacy and language skills in addition to mathematical skills. Teacher's will also continue to use online educational programs such as Raz- Kids, Waggle and Success Maker. The program will target 100 immigrant students who are also newcomer ELLs from both general education and special education classes. These students will be divided into 5 classes. Teachers will be using the workshop model to facilitate their instruction. The types of activities students are engaging in are the Four Hallmarks of Advanced Literacies to support the four modalities of language, where students are engaging in discussions, vocabulary support and writing tasks are aligned to a challenging on grade level text. Teachers will implement small group instruction where needed and all activities are differentiated to provide access to all students. The program will be supervised by a site supervisor and in addition, student attendance is being weekly monitored via a digital sheet that teacher's access using their school's 143q.in domain. All program notifications are translated in different languages via school staff. Throughout each session for each of the Title III Immigrant Programs, students will engage in hands-on, collaborative activities that support their academic and language growth. Activities in these programs will promote cognitive development aligned to the Next Generation Learning Standards and foster the use of metacognitive strategies, including self-assessment, monitoring tools, and student rubrics. Instructional strategies taught in these programs will include activating background knowledge, contextualizing new information through realia, utilizing graphic supports and verbal analogies, and building schema through meaningful connections to students’ lived experiences. These activities are intentionally designed to enhance instructional opportunities for immigrant students who may be adjusting to a new educational system in the United States. Instruction will support students’ cultural adjustment by providing opportunities for students to share their experiences, cultures, and prior knowledge as part of classroom discussions and project-based learning activities. Teachers will intentionally build background knowledge for students who are new to U.S. schools by explicitly teaching academic vocabulary, modeling expectations for classroom discourse, and introducing foundational concepts that support understanding of U.S. academic content. For students with Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE) or those with limited exposure to grade-level academic content, instruction will incorporate scaffolds such as visual supports, sentence frames, structured discussion protocols, and differentiated reading and writing tasks. These scaffolds will ensure that all immigrant students can meaningfully participate in rigorous academic tasks while developing both language and content knowledge. PS 143’s Title III Immigrant funded programs is rooted in both qualitative observations and quantitative data that underscore the continued need to support English language acquisition and foundational literacy skills among our Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners (MLs/ELLs), with a particular focus on our Newcomer ELLs population. The Title III Immigrant Programs in our school focus on these modalities, so that students continue to improve their English language acquisition. These students are enrolled across ENL, Dual Language, and Transitional Bilingual Education programs, and include both English Proficient students and ELLs with special needs. Many of our students continue to experience the residual effects of interrupted learning due to the COVID-19 pandemic and school closures. As a result, targeted support is necessary to accelerate their language acquisition and strengthen foundational literacy skills. Project-Based Learning (PBL), language enrichment clubs, Saturday/Sunday Academy and language enriched theater programs have proven to be effective at PS 143, particularly in enhancing students’ English language development and overall engagement. Teachers have observed a notable increase in student participation and confidence in speaking, listening, reading and writing when engaged in PBL units and other enrichment activities. This approach provides students with authentic opportunities to use language meaningfully across content areas, promoting both academic and social-emotional growth. These outcomes affirm the need for a continued and expanded enrichment programs outside of the school day, to address the diverse linguistic needs of our ML/ELL population and ensure their progress toward English proficiency. Please make sure to describe: Professional development to be provided Activities that teachers will engage with by topic Whether the professional development provided will be at cost to Title III Immigrant funds The school’s professional development plan under the Title III Immigrant program is designed to strengthen the instructional practices of educators working with Multilingual and English Language Learners (ML/ELLs). The initiative focuses on equipping teachers with effective strategies and resources to better support the language development and academic success of their students. As part of the December/February/April Project-Based Learning Enrichment Camps, Saturday/Sunday Academy, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts) and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program, participating teachers will engage in ongoing training throughout the school year that emphasizes English as a New Language (ENL) skills, curriculum implementation, and ENL pedagogy. Teachers will receive targeted instruction in effective literacy strategies tailored to the needs of ELLs, ensuring that students build strong foundations in the modalities of listening, speaking, reading and writing. Training will also include sessions focused on utilizing project- based learning to support content, enhance classroom engagement, and continue language development. All professional development activities, including training sessions and related materials, will be at no cost to the Title III Immigrant funds. Through this comprehensive professional development plan, the school aims to build educator capacity and create a more inclusive, supportive, and effective learning environment for ML/ELL students. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 237 of 250 Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. For each activity include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for the professional development Data sources used to justify the professional development The rationale for the professional development provided under our Title III Immigrant program is rooted in both qualitative and quantitative data that highlight the need for targeted support in foundational literacy, ENL instructional strategies, and compliance with state mandates regarding ML/ELLs. Based on October 2025 RLAT results, our student population includes 154 students at the Entering level, 214 at the Emerging level, 247 at the Transitioning level, and 233 at the Expanding level. Additionally, October 2025 RLER data indicates that our school serves approximately 661 Newcomers and 185 Developing ELLs. To effectively serve our diverse student population, all teachers that work the Title III Immigrant programs, must be equipped with a strong understanding of ENL strategies, state regulations, and the ELL identification process. Professional development is therefore an ongoing, school-wide initiative delivered throughout the year by certified ENL teachers, dual language teachers, instructional supervisors, and our Bilingual/ENL Compliance point person, along with a DOE-contracted Orton-Gillingham (OG) consultant who provides structured literacy training. These sessions are strategically scheduled during designated common planning times and professional development periods, with monthly agendas and sign-in documentation to ensure accountability. When analyzing our data sources, such as the RLAT for our ELLs overall proficiency levels and proficiency in each modality, in addition to mCLASS data, we noticed that our ELLs need additional support in the modalities of Speaking and Writing. As a result, we host professional Development sessions, in which teachers analyze student ELL data to identify specific trends. Other sessions focus on the use of resources, scaffolds and strategies to promote speaking and writing in multiple, complete sentences. In addition, professional development is centered around student-led discussions, emphasizing vocabulary development and utilizing various structures. Staff learn how to implement a student-friendly NYSESLAT-aligned speaking rubric, which is a tool students can use to develop their proficiency in the speaking modality. To support students' development of writing skills, professional development sessions also focus on utilizing various graphic organizers, sentence structures, and creating a cohesive response when writing. The impact of professional development is measured qualitatively through PD self-reflections, ongoing in-house teacher surveys, and action plans, and quantitatively through analysis of student performance data, including mCLASS (K–5), MAP (K–5), and state exam results. Trends in our data—such as persistent challenges in foundational literacy skills observed through mCLASS and NYSESLAT results—indicate a clear need for continued professional learning in this area. Moreover, as our school works toward implementing school-wide protocols aligned with Hallmark #2 of the Instructional Focus—promoting talk and discussion to build both conversational and academic language—our Title III Immigrant students have demonstrated ongoing reading growth, further supporting the effectiveness of our PD sessions. In addition to foundational literacy and ENL-focused training, professional development includes sessions on project-based learning, where teachers explore the importance of hands-on, student-centered instruction that supports both academic content and language acquisition. These sessions guide educators in designing and implementing PBL experiences that encourage collaboration, critical thinking, and authentic use of language—key components in helping ML/ELLs develop both conversational and academic proficiency. Please specify how all teachers working in the Title III Immigrant program will receive ongoing professional development related to the Title III Immigrant program. All teachers involved in the Title III Immigrant- funded December/February/April Project-Based Learning Enrichment Camps, Saturday/Sunday Academy, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts), and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program will receive ongoing professional development focused on effective Project- Based Learning (PBL) strategies to better support ML/ELL students. This includes certified ENL teachers, general education teachers with ML/ELLs in their classrooms, dual language educators, and support staff. Professional development will focus on the design and implementation of project-based units that promote inquiry, collaboration, and meaningful language use. Teachers will explore how PBL fosters deeper engagement and accelerates language development by situating learning in real-world, culturally relevant contexts. Training sessions will include modeling of PBL practices, planning time, and collaborative reflection to ensure alignment with ENL instructional goals. By equipping all Title III ELL program educators with the tools to integrate PBL into their practice, we aim to create language-rich, student-centered learning environments that enhance both academic outcomes and language proficiency for our multilingual learners. As part of our ongoing commitment to supporting Multilingual and English Language Learners (ML/ELLs), our school’s professional development plan under the Title III Immigrant- funded program includes a structured calendar of targeted learning opportunities for educators throughout the year. The learning opportunities include Orton- Gillingham training, mCLASS data analysis, and utilizing ENL data from the NYSESLAT. These sessions are designed to build instructional capacity in foundational literacy, ENL pedagogy, curriculum implementation, and the use of data to inform instruction, with a particular emphasis on the integration of Project-Based Learning (PBL) strategies. These sessions are strategically scheduled during designated common planning times and professional development periods, with monthly agendas and sign-in documentation to ensure accountability. Throughout the year, all Title III Immigrant -funded program educators—including ENL and general education teachers, dual language educators, and support staff—will participate in these ongoing professional learning opportunities. By combining literacy-focused training, PBL integration, and data-driven reflection, this plan ensures that teachers are equipped to meet the diverse needs of ML/ELLs and foster both language development and academic achievement. To ensure thorough and accurate documentation of all professional development activities provided as part of our Title III Immigrant plan, our school will maintain a comprehensive system for organizing and storing all relevant records. For professional development sessions, we will collect and retain detailed agendas, sign-in sheets, presentation materials, and any handouts or resources distributed to participants. These documents will indicate the date, focus area, and target audience of each session. Additionally, we will maintain a system where copies of all documentation are stored and accessible in room L134. By systematically tracking and organizing this documentation, we will ensure that all professional development activities related to the Title III Immigrant programs are fully accounted for, aligned with program goals, and responsive to the instructional needs of our educators and the academic growth of our Multilingual Learners. These professional development sessions are at no cost to Title III Immigrant funds. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 242 of 250 Part D: Family Engagement Activities Part D: Family Engagement Activities Family Engagement Summary Provide a summary of your school’s family engagement activities under your Title III Immigrant program. Describe the activities that families of immigrant students (including ELLs) will engage with by topic. Please make sure to include whether the activities will be funded by Title III Immigrant funds. Please note: If family engagement activities will be at funded by Title III Immigrant funds, vendor cost is calculated as part of OTPS and must not exceed 20% of the total allocation; teacher per-session is calculated as part of family engagement and must not exceed 10% of the total allocation. Family engagement is a central component of our Title III Immigrant programs, rooted in the belief that strong partnerships between schools and families are essential for the academic, social, and emotional success of Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs). Our parent liaison and school counselors provide a robust series of monthly in-person workshops, designed to assist parents with ensuring their child's social, emotional, and academic success. Additionally, Coffee with the Principal/PTA is also offered monthly throughout the year to discuss various important topics related to the school and students. Workshops are led by a collaborative team that includes ENL teachers, site supervisors, and instructional coaches. Topics covered include understanding language acquisition stages, language development strategies, navigating school resources and supports, strategies to promote literacy at home, and social-emotional development. Prior to the launch of any Title III Immigrant program, families are invited to an orientation where they are informed of program goals, expectations, and the supports available to help their children succeed. Parents of students enrolled in the December/February/April Project-Based Learning Enrichment Camps, Saturday/Sunday Academy, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts), and Language Enriched Theater and the Arts program, will receive invitations at the end of each program to participate in the showcasing of projects presented by students. To celebrate our English Language Learners, honor their diverse cultural backgrounds, and support their unique needs, our school hosts a variety of parent engagement events. These include our Travel the World Cultural Fair, Painting for Students Celebration, and initiatives through our Newcomer Center. These events will be at cost to the Title III Immigrant funds. All parent engagement sessions are conducted in a welcoming, inclusive environment. Spanish-speaking staff are always on-site to provide interpretation, and Chinese interpretation is available upon request. For families who speak other languages, Over-the-Phone Interpretation (OPI) services are provided to ensure that all parents receive information in their preferred language. All communication, including invitations, reminders, and follow-up materials, are distributed in families’ home languages and maintained on file by our Parent Liaison, along with attendance sheets for each session. These workshops are at cost to Title III Immigrant funds. Through these efforts, we aim to foster a culturally responsive and inclusive school community in which families feel empowered, informed, and actively involved in their children's language development and academic success. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 243 of 250 Rationale for Family Engagement Activities Describe the rationale for the family engagement activities to be provided: For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., reasons for topics, time activities will be offered), and Data sources (e.g., parent survey responses, in-house parent surveys, PTA/PA request) used to justify the need for activities. To ensure that family engagement activities are purposeful and responsive to the needs of our multilingual learner (ML/ELL) families, our school relies on both quantitative and qualitative data sources. According to the September 2025 RFSF report, our student population includes 163 students at the Entering level, 208 at Emerging, 246 at Transitioning, and 234 at Expanding, based on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT and Fall 2025 NYSITELL assessments. These language proficiency levels highlight the importance of offering targeted support to families across a broad spectrum of English development. Parent interest and needs are identified through in-house surveys, teacher and staff feedback, and input from the PTA/PA. Survey responses collected at the beginning of the school year helped us determine topics of interest and preferences for engagement formats and times. One key area identified by families and staff was the need for a better understanding of the NYSESLAT exam. As a result, workshops were designed to introduce parents to the structure, purpose, and preparation strategies for the NYSESLAT across grade levels. These workshops provide opportunities for families to engage with sample materials and ask questions that support their child’s academic growth. To support language development through enrichment, we offer overviews for families of students participating in Project-Based Learning (PBL) Enrichment Camps, Saturday/Sunday Academy, Language Enrichment Clubs (Cheer, Music, Chess, Arts) and in our Language Enriched Theater and Arts program. These sessions explain how each program integrates English language development in fun, engaging ways aligned with student interests. Recognizing the unique needs of our recently arrived students and their families. In connection with our programs, families are invited to visit the school to view their children’s work, observe their academic progress, and gain a deeper appreciation of their development in English language skills. This experience strengthens home-school connections and encourages continued language learning at home. Parents of students enrolled in the programs will receive invitations at the end of each program to participate in showcases featuring student projects. To support our newly arrived students and their families, The Newcomers Center workshop is designed to address the unique needs of recently arrived students and their families, who often require additional support navigating the school system and accessing resources. Data from the NYSITELL assessments and registration interviews indicate a significant number of newcomers who benefit from targeted support. Feedback from teachers and parents has emphasized the importance of providing families with information about available resources, including language assistance and community connections. By equipping families with strategies to support their children’s language growth and engagement, the Newcomers Center fosters a welcoming and inclusive environment that promotes equity and belonging for all students. In addition to academic and informational workshops, we host our Travel the World Cultural Fair, a school-wide celebration that honors the diverse cultures within our community. Teachers host interactive booths that allow families to explore global traditions, art, music, food, and crafts while building cross-cultural understanding and community connection. This event also serves as a joyful way for families to see themselves and their cultures reflected in the school environment. To ensure equitable access and participation, all communications are provided in English, Spanish, and Chinese, with interpretation available in additional languages as needed. Family input is continually gathered through PTA/PA DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 244 of 250 meetings, “Coffee with the Principal” sessions, and one-on-one meetings with teachers. These platforms allow parents to share their ideas, questions, and concerns, which directly influence the planning and refinement of future events. The School Leadership Team includes parent representatives who collaborate with school staff to ensure family perspectives are consistently considered in school decision-making. Finally, we recognize the importance of building ongoing relationships between families and school staff. ENL teachers regularly meet with families during office hours and school events to discuss student progress, language goals, and individual successes. These experiences foster stronger home-school connections and encourage continued language learning beyond the classroom. Together, these thoughtfully planned activities reflect a comprehensive, data-informed approach to family engagement that values the voices, experiences, and contributions of all families in our school community. DBN: 24Q143 School: P.S. 143 Louis Armstrong 247 of 250 Families will be notified of Title III Immigrant- funded family engagement activities through multiple communication channels to ensure accessibility and inclusivity. Notifications will be shared via Smore newsletters, Class Dojo messages, multilingual flyers posted on the school’s official Instagram page, and invitations/letters sent home with students. All written communication will be translated into each family's preferred home language to ensure clear understanding and full participation. Additionally, interpretation services will be made available at all events to support real-time communication between staff and families, allowing parents and guardians to engage meaningfully in their children's educational experience. Many staff members speak Spanish, which is the language requested by most of our families. The staff members attend our family engagement events and provide real-time translation to families. Also, our presentations are translated into Spanish. Therefore, families can participate in engagement events by receiving information in a language they prefer. For other languages requested, we have staff member that speak Chinese, or we utilize over-the-phone translation services. This ensures that all families receive translation in their preferred language when attending family engagement events. Maintaining Records Describe how your school will maintain documentation (e.g. agendas, attendance sheets) of family engagement activities and notifications to families. To document outreach efforts, our school will maintain detailed records of all communications sent to families, including invitations, flyers, and emails. These materials will be kept in both English and the families’ preferred home languages to ensure accessibility and compliance with language access policies. Attendance at all family engagement activities will be recorded using sign-in sheets that track participant names, contact information, and the events attended. Additionally, records of interpretation and translation services provided during events—such as on-site interpreters or Over-the-Phone Interpretation—will be maintained, including the languages offered and the frequency of their use. This documentation will help ensure that all families receive appropriate language support and that the school can evaluate the effectiveness of its communication strategies. All records related to outreach, attendance, and interpretation services will be securely stored and readily available for program monitoring and compliance reviews.
